# Rationalism — Complete Learning Session

> **Syllabus, verbatim:** *Rationalism (Descartes, Spinoza, Leibniz); Cartesian Method and Certain Knowledge; Substance; God; Mind-Body Dualism; Determinism and Freedom* — UPSC Philosophy Paper I, Section A, item 2.
> **Evidence discipline:** ✅ marks doctrine on which the standard sources converge, ⚠️ marks analytical reconstruction for exam use, and ❓ marks a live scholarly dispute or a contested attribution. Spinoza is cited by structural reference (*Ethics* I, Definition 7; I, P15; II, P7) because those references are identical in every translation; Descartes is cited by Meditation and Leibniz by section of the *Monadology*. Model answers are independent learner practice and are never official UPSC keys.
> **Terminology rule:** the English concept leads and the original Latin or French term follows immediately — *methodic doubt (dubitatio)*, *thinking thing (res cogitans)*, *God, or Nature (Deus sive Natura)*, *striving (conatus)*, *monads have no windows (les monades n'ont point de fenêtres)*.

## BASIC LEARNING SESSION

### CONCEPT SPINE — ENGLISH FIRST, LATIN OR FRENCH IMMEDIATELY AFTER

```text
                 THE ONE QUESTION ALL THREE MEN ANSWER
      WHAT CAN REASON KNOW WITH NECESSITY, AND WHAT IS THERE TO KNOW?
                                  |
        SHARED PREMISE: experience may OCCASION a thought, but it cannot
        by itself supply necessity, universality or a first principle
                                  |
                 THE TEST THEY ALL INHERIT — WHAT IS A SUBSTANCE?
        "that which is in itself and is conceived through itself,
         needing nothing else in order to exist"
                                  |
   +------------------+-----------------------+------------------------+
   |                  |                       |                        |
 DESCARTES          SPINOZA                 LEIBNIZ                     |
 applies the test   applies the test        adds SIMPLICITY to          |
 LOOSELY            STRICTLY                the test                    |
   |                  |                       |                        |
 THREE substances   ONE substance           INFINITELY MANY substances  |
 God + thinking     God, or Nature          monads: simple, unextended, |
 thing (res         (Deus sive Natura)      perceiving, striving        |
 cogitans) +        thought and extension   bodies = well-founded       |
 extended thing     are ATTRIBUTES;         phenomena (phaenomena       |
 (res extensa)      minds and bodies        bene fundata)               |
   |                are MODES                 |                        |
   |                  |                       |                        |
 MIND AND BODY      MIND AND BODY           MIND AND BODY               |
 INTERACT           are ONE THING under     NEVER INTERACT              |
 (pineal gland)     TWO ATTRIBUTES          pre-established harmony     |
   |                  |                       |                        |
 GOD GUARANTEES     GOD IS THE SYSTEM       GOD SELECTS AND             |
 knowledge          immanent, not           HARMONISES the best of      |
 (no deceiver)      transitive, cause       all possible worlds         |
   |                  |                       |                        |
 FREEDOM =          FREEDOM = acting        FREEDOM = spontaneity +     |
 the will can       from one's own          intelligence + contingency  |
 affirm, deny or    understood nature       reasons "incline without    |
 withhold assent    (adequate ideas)        necessitating"              |
   +------------------+-----------------------+------------------------+
                                  |
     THE COST EACH PAYS: Descartes cannot explain interaction;
     Spinoza loses individuality and alternative possibilities;
     Leibniz must buy correspondence from God in advance
```

*Concept spine: read it downward. One inherited definition of substance, three different degrees of strictness in applying it, and everything else — God, mind, body and freedom — follows from that single decision.*

> 🔑 **Mnemonic — substance count "3 → 1 → ∞".** Descartes **3**, Spinoza **1**, Leibniz **∞**.
> 🔑 **Mnemonic — mind and body "I-P-H".** **I**nteraction → **P**arallelism → pre-established **H**armony.
> 🔑 **Mnemonic — what God does, "G-I-S".** For Descartes God **G**uarantees, for Spinoza God **I**s the system, for Leibniz God **S**elects.

### ANSWER-WORTHINESS — WHAT EARNS MARKS AND WHAT DOES NOT

| Classification | Content |
|---|---|
| **CORE MAINS** | The basic tenets of rationalism; methodic doubt and the thinking self (*cogito*); clear and distinct perception; the three kinds of ideas and innate ideas against Locke; the wax argument; the three proofs of God and the Cartesian Circle; the proof of the external world; substance in all three thinkers; God in all three thinkers; interaction, parallelism and pre-established harmony; determinism, necessity and the three accounts of freedom |
| **CORE SUPPORTING** | Spinoza's three kinds of knowledge and the fourth-proportional example; striving (*conatus*) and the passive/active affects, exactly as far as they explain freedom; monadic perception, appetition and hierarchy; the identity of indiscernibles; truths of reason and truths of fact; the best of all possible worlds; the comparative tables; translation and provenance discipline |
| **SUPPORTING** | The four rules of method; "hats and coats"; the two-clocks image; the thrown-stone image; *natura naturans* and *natura naturata*; the acosmism charge; Voltaire's *Candide* as reception, not as doctrine |
| **OPTIONAL ADVANCED** | The Cartesian-circle interpretive literature; the exact status of Spinoza's unknown attributes; contingency by infinite analysis; the panentheism-versus-pantheism labelling dispute |
| **NOT OWNED HERE** | Locke's, Berkeley's and Hume's positive systems (Topic 03); Kant's critical synthesis and his refutation of the ontological proof as a *doctrine* (Topic 04); Descartes' detailed physiology, optics and cosmology; Spinoza's biblical criticism, political theory and full taxonomy of the affects; Leibniz's calculus disputes, relational theory of space and time and the full architecture of the *Theodicy* |

⚠️ **Ownership boundary, stated once.** Locke appears here **only** as the named counter-argument the 2023 paper demands, and Kant appears **only** as a one-line evaluative rider on the ontological proof. Both are legitimate cross-links; neither is marks-essential content in this item, and neither should displace Descartes, Spinoza or Leibniz from the page.

### ORIGIN AND CHRONOLOGY — THE MINIMUM NEEDED TO AVOID ANACHRONISM

Rationalism is not three separate biographies. It is one argument that runs for roughly eighty years, in which each thinker reads his predecessor and tightens the same definition.

```text
 1596-1650  RENE DESCARTES
   1637  Discourse on the Method (French)  -> the four rules; "I think, therefore
                                              I am" (je pense, donc je suis)
   1641  Meditations on First Philosophy (Latin; French 1647)
           Meditation I   -> senses, dream, evil genius (genius malignus)
           Meditation II  -> "I am, I exist"; the wax; mind better known than body
           Meditation III -> the causal or "trademark" proof of God
           Meditation IV  -> error: the will outruns the intellect
           Meditation V   -> the ontological proof
           Meditation VI  -> the real distinction; the external world
   1641  Objections and Replies -> ARNAULD states the Circle (Fourth Objections)
   1643  Correspondence with PRINCESS ELISABETH OF BOHEMIA -> the interaction problem
   1644  Principles of Philosophy -> "substance" defined and conceded to be analogical
   1649  Passions of the Soul -> the pineal gland and the mind-body union
                                    |
 1632-1677  BENEDICT DE SPINOZA          (reads Descartes; refuses the looseness)
   1663  Descartes' Principles of Philosophy, set out geometrically
   1670  Theological-Political Treatise (published anonymously)
   1674  LETTER 50 to Jarig Jelles -> "determination is negation"
   1677  Ethics (posthumous), demonstrated in geometrical order (more geometrico)
           Part I   -> substance, God or Nature, necessity, immanent cause
           Part II  -> attributes, modes, parallelism, the three kinds of knowledge
           Part III -> striving (conatus) and the affects
           Part IV  -> human bondage; Deus sive Natura appears in the Preface
           Part V   -> freedom, intuitive knowledge, intellectual love of God
                                    |
 1646-1716  GOTTFRIED WILHELM LEIBNIZ    (reads both; refuses the single substance)
   1686  Discourse on Metaphysics -> the complete individual concept
   1704  New Essays on Human Understanding -> written against Locke (published 1765)
   1710  Theodicy -> the best of all possible worlds (le meilleur des mondes possibles)
   1714  Monadology (French; printed 1720/1840) -> §7 "monads have no windows"
   1715-16  Leibniz-Clarke correspondence -> sufficient reason; indiscernibles
                                    |
   1759  Voltaire, Candide -> satirises "the best of all possible worlds"
         ⚠️ reception, not doctrine: never cite Candide as Leibniz's own position
```

- ✅ The order is causal, not merely chronological. Spinoza's monism is a *correction* of the equivocation Descartes himself concedes in the *Principles*; Leibniz's pluralism is a *correction* of Spinoza's absorption of individuals.
- ⚠️ **Two cautions.** First, the *Ethics* was published only after Spinoza's death, so contemporary reaction to "Spinozism" often ran ahead of the text. Second, the *Monadology* was written in 1714 but printed later, so "Leibniz's system" must be assembled from the *Discourse*, the *Theodicy*, the *Monadology* and the correspondence together — a point that matters when an examiner quotes one work.

This is a route map, not a substitute for the twelve complete Basic subtopics below.

### SESSION 1 — WHAT MAKES RATIONALISM A SCHOOL, AND WHY THE THREE SYSTEMS DIVERGE

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Rationalism is the view that the mind's own resources — not the senses — are what make certain, necessary and universal knowledge possible, and that reality is built so that reason can reach it.

**Technical definition:** Early-modern rationalism is the doctrine that some concepts and principles are innate or self-evident to the intellect, that intellectual intuition and deduction rather than sense-experience supply the warrant for necessity and universality, that reality has an intelligible rational structure, and that this structure is to be expressed through a metaphysics of substance, attribute and mode.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Rationalism is not the denial of experience but a claim about *warrant*: experience may occasion a thought, yet only the intellect can certify that a truth is necessary and universal — and because Descartes, Spinoza and Leibniz enforce that single claim with different degrees of strictness on one inherited definition of substance, the same method yields three incompatible worlds, of three substances, of one, and of infinitely many.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **a priori warrant** — necessity and universality, not mere generalisation
- **innate cognitive resources** — native capacities, not preformed conscious propositions
- **intellectual intuition and deduction**
- **intelligibility of the real** — the real is the rational
- **substance, attribute and mode**
- **the "3 → 1 → ∞" sequence**

**How to use them:** Open with warrant rather than with a caricature of the senses; state the five tenets; then use the substance test to show that the divergence is *internal to* rationalism, not evidence against it.

#### Plain-language visual — why "the senses are enough" is not enough

```text
   WHAT EXPERIENCE CAN GIVE            WHAT REASON IS ASKED TO GIVE
   +--------------------------+        +----------------------------------+
   | I have seen many         |  --->  | EVERY triangle, seen or unseen,  |
   | triangles; each had       |        | in every possible case, MUST     |
   | angles summing to 180     |        | have angles summing to 180       |
   +--------------------------+        +----------------------------------+
   "so far, always"                     "necessarily, and I can see why"
             |                                        |
             +----------------------------------------+
                                |
        THE GAP: no number of observed cases turns "so far" into "must".
        The rationalist says the intellect closes that gap; the empiricist
        says the gap cannot be closed and the "must" is a habit of mind.
```

**In simple words:** everyone agrees that we *learn* about triangles by drawing them. The dispute is about what makes the conclusion *binding*. A rationalist says the binding force comes from the intellect grasping why the thing could not be otherwise. That is the whole quarrel in one line, and it is the line that must open a rationalism answer.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Occasion vs warrant | sense may *start* the thought; intellect *licenses* it |
| Necessary | could not be otherwise |
| Universal | holds for all cases, not for all observed cases |
| *A priori* | justified independently of any particular experience |
| Not the claim | that the senses always deceive or that the world is unreal |

> **One-line memory:** rationalism is a thesis about **warrant**, not a phobia about **eyes**.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — the five tenets and the single test that splits the school

```text
   THE FIVE TENETS THAT DEFINE THE SCHOOL
   =====================================================================
   1 INNATE COGNITIVE RESOURCES  some concepts/principles are native
                                 capacities of intellect, not copies
                                 taken from sensation
   2 REASON GROUNDS NECESSITY    experience occasions; intellect warrants
   3 INTUITION AND DEDUCTION     Descartes: clear intuition + ordered
                                 deduction | Spinoza: geometrical order
                                 (more geometrico) | Leibniz: identities,
                                 sufficient reasons, complete concepts
   4 THE REAL IS THE RATIONAL    reality has an intelligible structure
   5 SUBSTANCE METAPHYSICS       explain by substance, attribute, mode
   =====================================================================
                                |
                                v
        ONE INHERITED DEFINITION IS PUT TO WORK ON TENET 5:
        substance = in itself, conceived through itself, needing
                    nothing else in order to exist
                                |
        HOW STRICTLY DO YOU ENFORCE "NEEDING NOTHING ELSE"?
                                |
   +----------------+-----------+-----------------+
   | LOOSELY        | STRICTLY  | STRICTLY, PLUS  |
   | (analogically) |           | SIMPLICITY      |
   | DESCARTES: 3   | SPINOZA:1 | LEIBNIZ: infinity|
   +----------------+-----------+-----------------+
                                |
                                v
        EVERYTHING ELSE FOLLOWS: what God is, whether mind and body
        interact, and what freedom can possibly mean
```

⚠️ **Caption.** The five tenets are the *shared* content; the single question under them is where the school breaks. An answer that lists the tenets and stops has given the examiner half a page of agreement and no explanation of the disagreement.

#### 1. The five tenets, stated exactly

✅ The following table is the answer to the first limb of the 2025 twenty-mark question.

| Tenet | Content | The precision that earns the mark |
|---|---|---|
| **Innate cognitive resources** | Some concepts and principles are native capacities or structures of the intellect rather than copies derived from sensation | Say *capacities or dispositions*, not "truths known at birth" — otherwise Locke's objection lands before you have finished the sentence |
| **Reason as the ground of necessity** | Experience may occasion thought, but the intellect supplies the warrant for necessity, universality and first principles | The word to use is **warrant**, not "source of ideas" |
| **Intuition and deduction** | Descartes uses clear intellectual intuition and ordered deduction; Spinoza adopts geometrical demonstration (*more geometrico*); Leibniz reasons through identities, sufficient reasons and complete concepts | Naming the three *different* methods shows you are not writing a school summary |
| **The intelligibility of reality** | The real is the rational: the structure of the world is accessible to reason | This is the tenet that licenses metaphysics at all |
| **Substance metaphysics** | Reality is explained in terms of substance or substances, their attributes and their modes | This is the tenet that splits the school, so introduce it last |

❌ **Rationalism is not** the claim that all sensory beliefs are false, that the senses are useless, or that every truth is consciously present in the mind at birth. Each of those is a caricature, and each is refuted in one line by an examiner.

#### 2. The one definition all three inherit

✅ **Substance** is *that which exists in itself, is conceived through itself, and needs nothing else in order to exist.*

The definition contains a hidden dial: **how much dependence is compatible with "needing nothing else"?**

- Descartes turns the dial **down**. God alone needs absolutely nothing; but mind and matter need only God, so they may be called substances in a *dependent* or *created* sense. He concedes the equivocation himself in the *Principles of Philosophy* I.51.
- Spinoza turns the dial **up to maximum**. If a thing is conceived through itself, it cannot depend on anything at all — so only the infinite qualifies, and there is exactly one such thing.
- Leibniz keeps Descartes' plurality but adds a second requirement, **simplicity**: what is extended is divisible, what is divisible is composite, and a composite is not ultimate. So the real units must be partless, and there are infinitely many of them.

> 🔑 **Memory chain:** **attribute separates → independence unifies → simplicity multiplies.**

#### 3. Why a shared method does not force a shared metaphysics

⚠️ This is the mechanism the 2018 twenty-mark question asks for, and it must be stated as a *mechanism*, not as a table.

1. A method tells you **how** to argue: begin from what is evident, proceed in order, admit nothing unexamined.
2. A method does not tell you **which** criterion of "ontologically basic" to enforce, nor how strictly.
3. Descartes, Spinoza and Leibniz enforce different criteria — real distinction by principal attribute, absolute independence, and simplicity with internal activity.
4. ∴ Their disagreement is *produced by* rationalism operating consistently, not by one of them abandoning it.

#### 4. The three consequences that follow immediately

| Decision on substance | What God must then be | What mind and body must then be | What freedom can then mean |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Descartes — three** | a transcendent creator who guarantees knowledge | two really distinct substances that nevertheless interact | the will's power to affirm, deny or withhold assent |
| **Spinoza — one** | the one immanent substance, God, or Nature (*Deus sive Natura*) | one and the same thing under two attributes | acting from one's own understood nature |
| **Leibniz — infinitely many** | the necessary being who selects and harmonises the best world | two series of monadic states that correspond without interacting | spontaneity, intelligence and contingency together |

✅ Read the table across, not down. Each row is a single decision worked out to its end, which is why answers that treat "God", "mind–body" and "freedom" as three unrelated questions never reach the top band.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** Rationalism is a thesis about the warrant for necessary truth, and its internal disagreements are generated by one shared definition of substance rather than by any departure from the method.
> **Named evidence.** All three inherit the formula that substance is what exists in itself and is conceived through itself; Descartes concedes in *Principles* I.51 that only God satisfies it without qualification, yet still admits created substances, while Spinoza's *Ethics* I refuses that concession and Leibniz's *Monadology* §§1–3 adds simplicity as a further requirement.
> **Analysis.** The concession is the hinge. Once "needing nothing else" is allowed to mean "needing nothing except God", the number of substances becomes a matter of how strictly the phrase is read — and Spinoza and Leibniz simply read it more strictly in two different directions.
> **Qualification.** The mechanism explains the divergence without settling it: rationalism supplies the deductive ambition and the vocabulary, but it contains no neutral procedure for deciding which criterion of substancehood is the correct one, and that is the honest limit of the school.

#### Exam link

- **2025 Q2(a), 20 marks** — "the basic tenets of Rationalism" is answered from §1, and the second limb is answered by carrying those tenets stage by stage through Descartes in Sessions 2 to 6.
- **2018 Q2(a), 20 marks** — "the reason for the difference" is answered from §2 and §3; the examiner is asking for the dial, not for the three positions.
- Every comparative question in the paper opens more strongly if §4 is stated as a single decision worked out three times.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| "Rationalists reject sense-experience" | They reject sense-experience as a *warrant* for necessity; Descartes rebuilds the physical world and Spinoza calls sense the first kind of knowledge, not a nullity |
| "Innate ideas means babies know geometry" | Innateness is dispositional — a native capacity elicited on an appropriate occasion |
| Listing the tenets and stopping | The 2025 question has two limbs; the tenets are the smaller one |
| Treating the three thinkers as three biographies | The examiner rewards the mechanism that connects them |
| "Rationalism is Western logic, empiricism is Western observation" | Both are theories of justification; the contrast is about warrant, not about temperament |

#### Revision notes

- Five tenets: innate resources; reason grounds necessity; intuition and deduction; the real is the rational; substance metaphysics.
- One definition: in itself, conceived through itself, needing nothing else.
- The dial: loosely → 3; strictly → 1; strictly plus simplicity → ∞.
- Method fixes *how* to argue, not *which* criterion to enforce.
- Substance decision drives God, mind–body and freedom in one movement.
- Never define the school by hostility to the senses.

#### CLOSING RECALL FLOW — WHAT MAKES RATIONALISM A SCHOOL

```text
NECESSITY AND UNIVERSALITY NEED A WARRANT EXPERIENCE CANNOT SUPPLY
        |
        v
FIVE TENETS: innate resources | reason warrants | intuition+deduction |
             the real is rational | substance metaphysics
        |
        v
ONE INHERITED DEFINITION OF SUBSTANCE -> HOW STRICT IS "NEEDS NOTHING ELSE"?
        |
        +--> LOOSE (analogical)   -> DESCARTES 3 -> interaction problem
        +--> STRICT               -> SPINOZA  1  -> individuality problem
        +--> STRICT + SIMPLICITY  -> LEIBNIZ  oo -> harmony must be bought from God
        |
        v
GOD, MIND-BODY AND FREEDOM ARE NOT THREE TOPICS BUT ONE DECISION WORKED OUT
TRAP: not anti-sense | not innate propositions | not three biographies
ANSWER LINE: a shared method underdetermines the metaphysics, and that is
             rationalism's honest limit
```

### SESSION 2 — DESCARTES I: THE METHOD, METHODIC DOUBT AND THE THINKING SELF

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Descartes deliberately pretends that everything doubtful is false, in order to find out whether anything at all survives — and what survives is the thinking that is doing the doubting.

**Technical definition:** Methodic or hyperbolic doubt (*dubitatio hyperbolica*) is a foundationalist device that suspends assent to whole classes of belief — the sensory, the perceptual-world-involving and even the mathematical — in order to isolate an indubitable first truth, which Descartes finds in the performative self-verification of "I am, I exist" whenever it is thought.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Descartes' doubt is not scepticism but scepticism turned into an instrument: it is pushed to the point of self-destruction, because the one proposition it cannot touch is the existence of the thinker who is performing it — and that single performative certainty, not any argument, is the foundation on which the whole rationalist reconstruction is built.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **methodic and hyperbolic doubt** — deliberate, universal, provisional
- **the four rules of method** (*Discourse on the Method*, Part II)
- **sense-deception, the dream argument, the evil genius (*genius malignus*)**
- **"I am, I exist"** — the *Meditations* formulation
- **"I think, therefore I am" (*cogito, ergo sum*)** — the *Discourse* and *Principles* formulation
- **immediate intuition, not a syllogism**
- **the Archimedean point**

**How to use them:** Name the three stages of doubt in order of increasing power, then show that the third stage generates its own defeat, and only then state the *cogito* — with its exact status as an intuition.

#### Plain-language visual — a ladder of doubt that eats its own last rung

```text
   RUNG 1  THE SENSES SOMETIMES DECEIVE
           a straight stick looks bent in water; a tower looks round
           -> so sensory beliefs are not certain
           BUT: "I am sitting here holding this paper" still feels safe
                                |
   RUNG 2  THE DREAM ARGUMENT
           there is no mark by which I can tell waking from dreaming
           -> so the WHOLE external world may be absent
           BUT: 2 + 3 = 5 whether I am awake or asleep
                                |
   RUNG 3  THE EVIL GENIUS (genius malignus)
           suppose an omnipotent deceiver makes me err even in arithmetic
           -> so even mathematics is suspended
           BUT: to be deceived, I must EXIST to be deceived
                                |
                                v
                 THE LADDER BREAKS UNDER ITS OWN WEIGHT
                 "I am, I exist" is true whenever I think it
```

**In simple words:** each rung is stronger than the last, and Descartes builds them in that order on purpose. The evil genius is not a horror story; it is the strongest possible doubt, invented so that whatever survives *it* cannot be doubted at all. And the deceiver argument is exactly the one that cannot work, because there must be somebody there to be fooled.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Methodic | adopted deliberately, as a procedure |
| Hyperbolic | pushed further than ordinary life warrants |
| Provisional | Descartes does not *believe* the world is absent |
| Target | not "many beliefs" but whole *classes* of belief at once |
| Result | one proposition that resists the strongest doubt |

> **One-line memory:** the doubt is a **tool**, and the *cogito* is what the tool cannot cut.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — the method, the doubt and the foundation as one movement

```text
  THE FOUR RULES (Discourse on the Method, Part II, 1637)
  +------------------------------------------------------------------+
  | 1 EVIDENCE    accept nothing as true unless clearly and           |
  |               distinctly evident; avoid haste and prejudice       |
  | 2 ANALYSIS    divide each difficulty into as many parts as        |
  |               possible and as are required to solve it            |
  | 3 ORDER       proceed from the simplest and easiest objects       |
  |               upward to the more complex, positing order even     |
  |               where none naturally precedes                       |
  | 4 ENUMERATION make enumerations so complete and reviews so        |
  |               general that nothing is omitted                     |
  +------------------------------------------------------------------+
                    | rule 1 applied without mercy
                    v
  CLASS-BY-CLASS DEMOLITION (Meditation I)
      senses  ---------> unreliable in some cases -> withhold assent
      the world -------> dream argument -> withhold assent
      mathematics -----> evil genius -> withhold assent
                    |
                    v
  THE RESIDUE (Meditation II)
      "I am, I exist, is necessarily true whenever it is put forward
       by me or conceived in my mind"
                    |
        +-----------+-------------------------------+
        |                                           |
  WHAT IT PROVES                            WHAT IT DOES NOT PROVE
  that thinking exists now                  an enduring immaterial soul
  that I exist while thinking               personal identity over time
  a truth immune to the strongest doubt     that I am ONLY a thinking thing
                    |
                    v
  THE ARCHIMEDEAN POINT: one fixed place from which the rest is moved
```

⚠️ **Caption.** Read the two lower boxes together. Almost every avoidable mark loss on this topic comes from letting the left box quietly expand into the right box.

#### 1. The four rules — and why rule 1 does all the damage

✅ The four rules of the *Discourse on the Method*, Part II are: accept nothing that is not evidently true; divide each problem into parts; proceed in order from simple to complex; make enumerations complete.

⚠️ Rules 2 to 4 are ordinary intellectual hygiene, borrowed from mathematical practice. Rule 1 is the explosive one: if assent is to be withheld from whatever is not *evident*, and if a single counterexample makes a class of beliefs non-evident, then whole classes must go at once. The *Meditations* is rule 1 executed without mercy.

#### 2. The three stages of doubt, in their strict order

| Stage | Argument | What it removes | What still survives |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Sense-deception** | the senses have deceived me before — about a bent stick, a distant tower — and it is imprudent to trust wholly what has deceived even once | the reliability of particular perceptual judgements | apparently, my immediate situation: this hand, this paper |
| **The dream argument** | there are no certain marks by which waking can be distinguished from sleep | the immediate situation too, and with it the whole external world | the simple and universal — extension, shape, quantity, number; and mathematics |
| **The evil genius (*genius malignus*)** | suppose not a good God but a supremely powerful malicious deceiver devotes all his effort to deceiving me | even arithmetic and geometry; even "2 + 3 = 5" | only what cannot be false while I am thinking it |

✅ The order is cumulative and deliberate: each stage attacks what the previous stage left standing. Reporting the three arguments in any other order shows the structure has not been understood.

#### 3. The *cogito* — its exact status

✅ In the *Discourse* (1637) Descartes writes *je pense, donc je suis*, and the Latin tag *cogito, ergo sum* appears in the *Principles* I.7. In the *Meditations* the formulation is different and more careful: **"I am, I exist, is necessarily true whenever it is put forward by me or conceived in my mind."**

- ✅ It is **not a syllogism**. Descartes explicitly denies that it is deduced from a suppressed major premise such as "everything that thinks exists"; he says it is grasped by a simple intuition of the mind.
- ✅ Its force is **performative and present-tense**. It is not the claim that thought guarantees existence in general, but that the very act of entertaining the doubt certifies the existence of the doubter at that moment.
- ✅ It defeats the evil genius by *using* him: "let him deceive me as much as he can, he can never bring it about that I am nothing so long as I think I am something."
- ⚠️ It is the **Archimedean point** — Descartes' own image of one firm and immovable point sufficient to move the earth.

#### 4. What the *cogito* does not yet establish

⚠️ This is the single most examined limit in the whole topic, and it is where the 2022 question is won or lost.

1. It establishes that **thinking exists**, and that the thinker exists *while thinking*. It does not by itself establish an **enduring immaterial substance**.
2. It does not establish **diachronic personal identity** — that the thinker now is the same thinker as a moment ago.
3. It does not establish that the thinker is **only** a thinking thing; the real distinction of mind from body is argued much later, in Meditation VI.
4. Descartes therefore spends the rest of Meditation II developing the **thinking thing (*res cogitans*)** — "a thing that doubts, understands, affirms, denies, is willing, is unwilling, and also imagines and has sensory perceptions."

❓ Later critics press exactly here: Lichtenberg's remark that Descartes was entitled only to "there is thinking", and Hume's bundle theory of the self, both attack the slide from an occurring thought to a substantial thinker. Naming that gap *and* saying that Descartes takes further steps to close it is the mark of a controlled answer.

#### 5. Why this is the *rationalist* foundation and not just a clever puzzle

⚠️ Three features make the *cogito* a rationalist starting point rather than a sceptical curiosity:

- it is reached **without any sensory premise** — it survives the hypothesis that no body and no world exists;
- it is grasped by **intellectual intuition**, the very faculty tenet 3 of Session 1 privileges;
- it supplies the **model** from which the criterion of truth is read off in Session 3 — Descartes asks what it is about this proposition that makes it certain, and answers: it is clearly and distinctly perceived.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** Cartesian doubt is an instrument of certainty rather than an expression of scepticism, because it is designed to isolate a proposition that the strongest possible doubt cannot reach.
> **Named evidence.** Meditation I proceeds through sense-deception, the dream argument and the hypothesis of an evil genius (*genius malignus*); Meditation II then finds that "I am, I exist" is necessarily true whenever it is put forward or conceived, and the *Principles* I.7 records the shorter tag *cogito, ergo sum*.
> **Analysis.** The deceiver hypothesis defeats itself: deception presupposes somebody deceived, so the very supposition that maximises doubt guarantees the existence of the doubter. This is why Descartes insists the *cogito* is an immediate intuition and not an inference from "whatever thinks exists".
> **Qualification.** What is secured is narrower than it looks. The *cogito* certifies present thinking existence, not an enduring immaterial substance or personal identity through time; Descartes needs the rest of Meditation II and the real distinction of Meditation VI for that, which is precisely the gap Lichtenberg and Hume later exploit.

#### Exam link

- **2022 Q4(b), 15 marks** — the whole first limb, "certainty with reference to knowledge of the self", is §2 to §4; the second limb needs Session 5.
- **2025 Q2(a), 20 marks** — the *cogito* is stage one of "how Descartes builds a system in consonance with" the tenets.
- **2019 Q1(d), 10 marks** — this session supplies the starting point from which the proof of other things must begin.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| Calling the *cogito* a syllogism | Descartes explicitly denies a suppressed major premise; it is an intuition |
| Attributing "*cogito, ergo sum*" to the *Meditations* | The *Meditations* has "I am, I exist"; the Latin tag is from the *Discourse* and *Principles* I.7 |
| Making the *cogito* prove an immortal soul at once | It proves thinking existence; substantiality comes later and separately |
| Describing Descartes as a sceptic | The doubt is methodic, hyperbolic and provisional |
| Giving the three doubts in the wrong order | The order is cumulative and is itself an argument |

#### Revision notes

- Four rules: evidence, analysis, order, enumeration; rule 1 drives the demolition.
- Doubt in three cumulative stages: senses → dream → evil genius.
- *Meditations* wording: "I am, I exist … whenever it is put forward by me or conceived in my mind".
- Status: immediate intuition, performative, present-tense; not a syllogism.
- Limit: thinking existence, not enduring substance or identity through time.
- Archimedean point: one fixed place from which everything else is moved.

#### CLOSING RECALL FLOW — DESCARTES I: DOUBT AND THE THINKING SELF

```text
FOUR RULES -> rule 1: admit only the evident
        |
        v
SENSE-DECEPTION -> DREAM ARGUMENT -> EVIL GENIUS (genius malignus)
        | each stage attacks what the last left standing
        v
DECEPTION PRESUPPOSES SOMEONE DECEIVED
        |
        v
"I AM, I EXIST" whenever it is thought  |  intuition, NOT a syllogism
        |
        +--> thinking thing (res cogitans): doubts, understands, affirms,
        |    denies, wills, imagines, seems to sense
        |
        v
ARCHIMEDEAN POINT -> the criterion of truth is read off it (Session 3)
LIMIT: present thinking certain | enduring substance and identity NOT yet
TRAP: not scepticism | not a syllogism | Meditations wording differs from the tag
ANSWER LINE: the doubt is an instrument that destroys itself on the one thing
             it cannot reach
```

### SESSION 3 — DESCARTES II: CLEAR AND DISTINCT PERCEPTION, THE THREE KINDS OF IDEAS, AND INNATE IDEAS AGAINST LOCKE

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Having found one certain truth, Descartes asks what made it certain, generalises the answer into a rule, then sorts his ideas by where they come from and argues that the most important ones come from the mind's own nature.

**Technical definition:** Clear and distinct perception (*perceptio clara et distincta*) is Descartes' criterion of truth, abstracted from the *cogito* as its model; ideas are classified as adventitious (*adventitiae*), factitious (*factitiae*) and innate (*innatae*), and innateness is understood dispositionally as a native capacity of rational cognition rather than as a preformed conscious proposition.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Descartes does not assert innate ideas as an article of faith; he reaches them by an argument from insufficiency — finite, changing sensation cannot be the adequate source of the ideas of infinity, of substance or of exact mathematical necessity — and the strongest version of his claim, that innateness is a disposition rather than a preformed proposition, is precisely the version Locke's universal-assent objection does not touch.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **clear and distinct perception** — the criterion of truth
- **adventitious, factitious and innate ideas**
- **dispositional innateness** — "seeds of knowledge", not conscious propositions
- **objective reality and formal reality** of an idea
- **Locke's tests: universal assent; the transparency of mental content; sensation and reflection**
- **the *Essay concerning Human Understanding*, Book I**

**How to use them:** State the criterion and where it comes from; give the threefold classification; state innateness in its *strongest* dispositional form; only then let Locke attack, and adjudicate.

#### Plain-language visual — three doors into the mind

```text
        WHERE DID THIS IDEA COME FROM?
                     |
   +-----------------+------------------+-----------------------+
   |                 |                                          |
 ADVENTITIOUS     FACTITIOUS                                 INNATE
 (adventitiae)    (factitiae)                                (innatae)
 seem to come     I make them up                             belong to the
 from outside     by combining                               mind's own
                  others                                     rational nature
 heat, colour,    a golden mountain,                         thought, God,
 the noise of     a siren, a unicorn                         infinity, substance,
 a passing cart                                              the axioms of
                                                             mathematics
   |                 |                                          |
   v                 v                                          v
 involuntary      voluntary                                  neither received
 and passive      and constructed                            nor invented:
                                                             ELICITED
```

**In simple words:** the first two doors are uncontroversial. Everyone agrees I receive a smell and can invent a unicorn. The fight is entirely about the third door — and the fight is won or lost on what "innate" is allowed to mean. If it means "the baby already believes it", Descartes loses immediately. If it means "the mind is so built that, given the occasion, it produces this", he does not.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Clear | present and open to an attentive mind |
| Distinct | so sharply separated from everything else that it contains nothing unclear |
| Adventitious | apparently received from outside |
| Factitious | made by me out of other ideas |
| Innate | native to the mind's rational nature, elicited rather than copied |

> **One-line memory:** innate is a **capacity**, not a **memory**.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — the criterion, the classification and the argument from insufficiency

```text
  STEP 1  READ THE CRITERION OFF THE MODEL
  +--------------------------------------------------------------+
  | Why is "I am, I exist" certain?                              |
  | Because I perceive it CLEARLY and DISTINCTLY.                |
  | RULE: whatever I perceive very clearly and distinctly is true|
  +--------------------------------------------------------------+
            | (this rule is later put under strain: see the Circle,
            |  Session 5, and note that its GENERAL reliability is
            |  what God is brought in to secure)
            v
  STEP 2  SORT THE CONTENTS OF THE MIND
      ADVENTITIOUS -------- FACTITIOUS -------- INNATE
      passive, involuntary  voluntary, made    native capacity
            |                                       |
            v                                       v
  STEP 3  THE ARGUMENT FROM INSUFFICIENCY (why the third door is needed)
  +--------------------------------------------------------------+
  | An idea's OBJECTIVE REALITY (what it represents) must have a |
  | cause with at least as much FORMAL REALITY (what it is).     |
  |                                                              |
  |  IDEA OF THE INFINITE      cannot be assembled by adding to  |
  |                            or negating the finite, because   |
  |                            the finite is understood BY       |
  |                            CONTRAST with the infinite        |
  |  IDEA OF SUBSTANCE         no sense-organ delivers "bearer   |
  |                            of qualities"                     |
  |  MATHEMATICAL NECESSITY    no finite set of measured figures |
  |                            delivers "must, in every case"    |
  +--------------------------------------------------------------+
            |
            v
  STEP 4  STATE IT DISPOSITIONALLY, OR LOSE TO LOCKE IN ONE LINE
      "innate" = the mind is so constituted that on an appropriate
      occasion it produces the idea from its own resources
```

⚠️ **Caption.** Step 4 is the whole examination. Candidates who present Descartes at Step 2 and then report Locke's objections have staged a fight in which one side never got up.

#### 1. The criterion of truth, and where it comes from

✅ Descartes does not begin with the criterion; he *derives* it. He asks what it is about the *cogito* that makes it indubitable, finds that it is nothing but the clarity and distinctness of the perception, and generalises: **whatever I perceive very clearly and distinctly is true.**

- **Clear** — present and open to an attentive mind, as a plainly visible object is present to the eye.
- **Distinct** — so precisely separated from all other perceptions that it contains within itself only what is clear.

⚠️ Note the structure carefully, because it matters for the Circle in Session 5: at this point Descartes has a rule extracted from *one* instance. Turning it into a general guarantee for perceptions no longer being attended to is exactly the work God is later asked to do.

#### 2. The three kinds of ideas

| Kind | Latin | How it arises | Examples | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Adventitious** | *adventitiae* | seem to come to me from outside, involuntarily | heat, colour, the sound of a cart | passively received; their external cause is not yet established |
| **Factitious** | *factitiae* | constructed by me from other ideas | a golden mountain, a siren | voluntary; obviously mind-dependent |
| **Innate** | *innatae* | native to the mind's own rational nature | thought, God, infinity, substance, the axioms of mathematics | elicited by occasion, not copied from sense |

✅ The classification appears in Meditation III, and its point is to prepare the causal proof of God: the idea of God is placed in the third class, and its content is then argued to exceed anything a finite mind could have manufactured.

#### 3. The argument from insufficiency — why innate ideas are *needed*

⚠️ Do not present innate ideas as a bare assertion. Present them as the conclusion of an argument:

1. Some of my ideas have a content — an **objective reality**, in Descartes' technical vocabulary — that no finite, changing sensation could supply.
2. The idea of the **infinite** is the clearest case: it cannot be built by extending or negating the finite, because I recognise myself as finite only by contrast with an already-possessed idea of the infinite.
3. The idea of **substance** — a bearer that persists through changing qualities — is delivered by no sense-organ, as the wax argument in Session 4 shows directly.
4. **Mathematical necessity** cannot be read off any number of measured figures, because measurement yields "so far" and never "must".
5. ∴ These ideas must be native to the intellect's own constitution.

#### 4. Innateness stated at its strongest

✅ Descartes' own qualification is essential and is exactly what the 2023 question rewards: innateness need **not** mean an explicitly entertained proposition present in an infant's consciousness. It may be a **disposition** or native capacity, activated on an appropriate occasion — the mind carries "seeds of knowledge" which experience may prompt but does not create.

⚠️ Analogy Descartes himself uses in the *Comments on a Certain Broadsheet*: we say a family has an inherited disposition to a certain disease not because the infant suffers it in the womb but because the constitution is such that the condition will develop. Innateness is a constitution, not a content.

#### 5. Locke's three grounds of refutation, and the Cartesian replies

✅ Locke attacks innate principles in the *Essay concerning Human Understanding*, Book I, and offers the mind as "white paper, void of all characters", furnished from **sensation** and **reflection**.

| Locke's objection | The Cartesian reply | What still stands ❓ |
|---|---|---|
| **No universal assent.** Children and "idiots" — Locke's word — do not affirm the alleged innate principles; and a principle assented to by nobody in particular is assented to by nobody | Innateness is dispositional, not continuous conscious assent; universal *assent* was never the claim | A disposition must be specified narrowly enough that not *every* learnable truth counts as innate — Locke's sharpest residual point |
| **Nothing can be "in the mind" and unknown.** If mental content is transparent, an unperceived principle is not in the mind at all; on that standard every truth the mind can ever reach is already "in" it | Native capacities can operate before reflective recognition; the mind's structure is not the same as the mind's occurrent contents | The reply risks shifting from *innate ideas* to an uncontroversial *capacity to learn*, which is not a rationalist thesis at all |
| **Sensation and reflection suffice.** All the materials of ideas can be traced to experience and to the mind's observation of its own operations | Finite experience cannot by itself yield the positive idea of infinity or the warrant for mathematical necessity | Locke can reconstruct infinity by repetition and negation, and necessity by abstraction — whether that reconstruction succeeds is the live question |

⚠️ **The adjudication that earns the mark:** Locke decisively refutes *occurrent* innatism — the doctrine that the mind is born consciously holding propositions. He does not as clearly refute *dispositional* innatism. But the burden then shifts to the rationalist to say what makes a disposition specifically innate rather than merely a general capacity to learn, and that is the point at which the debate is genuinely unresolved.

#### 6. Where this feeds the system

- The criterion of truth from §1 is the rule that God will be asked to underwrite (Session 5).
- The innate **idea of God** from §2 and §3 is the premise of the causal proof (Session 5).
- The innate **idea of extension** is what the wax argument isolates (Session 4).
- The innate **idea of substance** is what makes the whole substance metaphysics of Session 6 available at all.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** Descartes' doctrine of innate ideas is an argument from the insufficiency of sensation, and it survives Locke only in its dispositional form.
> **Named evidence.** Meditation III divides ideas into adventitious, factitious and innate and locates the idea of God in the third class on the ground that its objective reality exceeds what a finite mind could supply; Locke's *Essay* I replies with the absence of universal assent among children, the transparency of mental content, and the sufficiency of sensation and reflection.
> **Analysis.** The two sides are not disagreeing about infants. Descartes' considered claim is that the mind's constitution produces certain ideas on an appropriate occasion, so absence of infant assent is simply irrelevant to it; Locke's real force lies in his second objection, which demands a criterion separating an innate disposition from a general capacity to learn.
> **Qualification.** On that demand Descartes is weaker than he needs to be: he shows convincingly that infinity and necessity are not copies of sensation, but he does not supply an independent test for innateness, so the doctrine ends the exchange defensible in its dispositional form and unproven in its strong form.

#### Exam link

- **2023 Q2(c), 15 marks** — this session *is* the answer: §2 to §4 for Descartes, §5 for Locke's grounds and the adjudication.
- **2025 Q2(a), 20 marks** — tenet 1, "innate cognitive resources", is instantiated here, and the criterion of §1 is the second stage of the system.
- **2019 Q1(d) and 2022 Q4(b)** — both depend on the criterion of §1 being in place before God is proved.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| "Innate ideas = knowledge present at birth" | Dispositional innateness: a native capacity elicited on occasion |
| Presenting Locke's objections without the strongest Descartes | The 2023 question asks for the theory *and* the grounds of refutation; a weak Descartes forfeits half the marks |
| Treating "clear and distinct" as a mere synonym for "obvious" | Clear = present to an attentive mind; distinct = containing nothing else |
| Saying Locke proves ideas come from the senses | Locke's sources are sensation **and reflection**; omitting reflection misstates him |
| Forgetting that the criterion is *derived* from the *cogito* | It is read off the model case, which is why its general reliability later needs God |

#### Revision notes

- Criterion: whatever is perceived clearly and distinctly is true; derived from the *cogito*, not assumed.
- Three kinds of ideas: adventitious, factitious, innate.
- Innate is dispositional — "seeds of knowledge", a constitution not a content.
- Argument from insufficiency: infinity, substance, mathematical necessity.
- Locke, *Essay* I: no universal assent; nothing unknown is in the mind; sensation and reflection suffice.
- Verdict: occurrent innatism refuted; dispositional innatism survives but under-specified.

#### CLOSING RECALL FLOW — DESCARTES II: IDEAS AND THE CRITERION

```text
WHY WAS THE COGITO CERTAIN? -> because clearly and distinctly perceived
        |
        v
RULE: whatever is perceived clearly and distinctly is true
        | (its GENERAL reliability is what God will underwrite)
        v
IDEAS SORTED: adventitious | factitious | INNATE
        |
        v
ARGUMENT FROM INSUFFICIENCY: infinity | substance | mathematical necessity
        | finite sensation cannot be their adequate cause
        v
INNATE = DISPOSITION (seeds of knowledge), not a preformed proposition
        |
        +--> LOCKE, Essay I: no universal assent | nothing unknown is in
        |    the mind | sensation and reflection suffice
        +--> REPLY: dispositions need no infant assent; capacities precede
        |    reflective recognition; infinity is not built from the finite
        v
VERDICT: occurrent innatism falls | dispositional innatism stands, but owes
         a criterion distinguishing it from a bare capacity to learn
TRAP: never defend the caricature; never omit Locke's "reflection"
ANSWER LINE: Locke refutes the version Descartes' best texts do not hold
```

### SESSION 4 — DESCARTES III: THE WAX ARGUMENT AND WHY BODY IS KNOWN BY INTELLECT

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Descartes takes the clearest possible physical object, melts it, notices that every sensible feature has changed while he still judges it to be the same thing, and concludes that what he grasps as "the wax" was never delivered by the senses at all.

**Technical definition:** The wax argument of Meditation II is a thought-experiment showing (a) that the identity of a body through total qualitative replacement is grasped by the intellect alone — by "mental scrutiny" (*inspectio mentis*) rather than by sense (*sensus*) or imagination (*imaginatio*) — (b) that the essence of body is extension, and (c) that the mind is better known (*notior*) than any body.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The wax argument is the missing half of "certain knowledge": the thinking self (*cogito*) tells Descartes *that* he exists, and the wax tells him *what kind* of thing he is and *how* bodies are known — for when every sensible property of the wax has been destroyed and the same wax is still judged to remain, what remains cannot be anything the senses ever supplied.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **the wax (*cera*)** — the clearest possible case of a body
- **mental scrutiny (*inspectio mentis*)** — rendered "purely mental scrutiny" in the Cottingham–Stoothoff–Murdoch translation
- **sense (*sensus*), imagination (*imaginatio*), understanding (*intellectus*)**
- **"flexible and changeable" (*flexibile et mutabile*)**
- **the extended thing (*res extensa*)**
- **"hats and coats"** — the paired case in the same Meditation
- **better known (*notior*)** — the printed sub-title of Meditation II

**How to use them:** Number the steps. The examiner is looking for a reconstruction, not for a story about a candle.

#### Plain-language visual — everything changes, and yet it is the same wax

```text
   BEFORE THE FIRE                        AFTER THE FIRE
   +--------------------------+           +--------------------------+
   | tastes of honey          |           | no taste                 |
   | smells of flowers        |  ------>  | no scent                 |
   | has a colour             |           | colour altered           |
   | has a shape and size     |           | shape and size lost      |
   | is hard and cold         |           | liquid and hot           |
   | makes a sound when rapped|           | makes no sound           |
   +--------------------------+           +--------------------------+
             |                                       |
             +------------------+--------------------+
                                |
                 EVERY item on the sensible list is gone
                 AND YET: "the same wax remains" - I judge this
                 without the least hesitation
                                |
                                v
                 SO WHAT WAS I GRASPING ALL ALONG?
                 Not any of the items. Something that survives
                 their total replacement.
```

**In simple words:** ask a friend what the wax *is*. They will list what they can see, smell and touch. Melt it, and every item on their list is false — yet nobody says a *new object* has appeared. That judgement of sameness is doing work no sense-organ could have done, and Descartes' entire point is to make that invisible work visible.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| The catalogue | taste, scent, colour, shape, size, hardness, coldness, sound |
| What survives | something extended, flexible and changeable |
| Who grasps it | the understanding (*intellectus*), not sense or imagination |
| The paired case | "hats and coats": I say I see men, but strictly I judge |
| The conclusion | the mind is *notior* — more evidently known — than the body |

> **One-line memory:** the wax does **not** prove that bodies exist; it proves *how* they would be known if they do.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — the eight steps, and the four presuppositions underneath them

```text
  1  TAKE THE CLEAREST CASE: wax fresh from the honeycomb
     sensible catalogue = taste | scent | colour | shape | size |
                          hardness | coldness | sound when rapped
                        |
  2  MOVE IT TO THE FIRE -> every item destroyed or replaced
                        |
  3  YET I JUDGE, WITHOUT HESITATION, THAT THE SAME WAX REMAINS
                        |
  4  THEREFORE what I take the wax to BE is not identical with any
     item at step 1 - all were replaced while the wax persisted
                        |
  5  NOR IS IT IMAGINATION: heat can give the wax an INDEFINITE
     number of shapes and sizes; I cannot run through indefinitely
     many images  (this is the force of flexibile et mutabile)
                        |
  6  THEREFORE the wax is grasped as an EXTENDED, FLEXIBLE,
     CHANGEABLE thing by the INTELLECT ALONE - by inspectio mentis
                        |
  7  FURTHER: at every stage - judging the wax present, doubting it,
     being deceived about it - I perceive MYSELF judging
                        |
  8  THEREFORE (a) bodies are known by understanding, not by sense
                (b) the mind is NOTIOR - more evidently known - than
                    any body, because it survives even if the body
                    does not exist

  ------------------- WHAT THE ARGUMENT ASSUMES ----------------------
  P1 SUBSTANCE/ATTRIBUTE METAPHYSICS IS ALREADY IN PLACE
     identity through total qualitative replacement needs a BEARER
     distinct from the qualities - step 3 uses the very metaphysics
     the Meditations claim to derive
  P2 SENSE IS EXHAUSTED BY THE CATALOGUE
     if sense could deliver a structural or relational item, step 4 fails
  P3 IMAGINATION IS IMAGE-BOUND and therefore finite in range,
     while intellect is not
  P4 PERCEPTION IS SELF-INTIMATING
     I cannot perceive without perceiving THAT I perceive - step 7
     depends wholly on this
```

⚠️ **Caption.** State the presuppositions. They are where the fifteen-mark and twenty-mark marks are, because they show you can see what an argument *costs* as well as what it delivers.

#### 1. The paired case in the same Meditation

✅ Descartes immediately supplies a second, homelier example: looking from a window he says he **sees men** crossing the square; strictly he sees only **hats and coats**, and *judges* that men are underneath. The moral is identical — what passes for seeing is in fact judging, an operation of the intellect.

⚠️ Use "hats and coats" when the answer needs a second illustration in one line. It is Descartes' own, it is memorable, and it shows the argument is not a one-off trick with a candle.

#### 2. What the argument establishes, exactly

| Claim | Established? | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Bodily identity through change is grasped by intellect | ✅ yes | This is the argument's main result |
| The essence of body is extension | ✅ yes, within this Meditation | Which is why Meditation VI's dualism is partly pre-installed here |
| The mind is better known than the body | ✅ yes, in the sense of *more indubitable* | Epistemic priority, not richer content |
| Bodies exist | ❌ **no** | External existence is restored only in Meditation VI by divine veracity |
| Knowledge of body is *a priori* | ❓ contested | Descartes shows the concept is not an image; he has not shown the knowledge is independent of experience |

#### 3. The three strongest objections, with replies

| Objection | Source | Reply | Residual ❓ |
|---|---|---|---|
| What survives melting is just "extended stuff", an **abstraction** built up from repeated sense-experience, not an intellectual intuition | Gassendi, *Fifth Objections*; the empiricist line generally | The abstraction still has to be *of* something identical across the change; the empiricist must name the sensory item that registers **identity**, and there is none | Descartes has shown the *concept* is not an image; he has not shown that the *knowledge* is a priori |
| Strip away every sensible quality and nothing remains to be known — "extension" without sensible qualities is unintelligible | Berkeley, *Principles* §§9–11 | Extension is not a further quality but the *mode of being* of every quality that could be sensed | The reply presupposes exactly what Berkeley denies |
| Step 7 confuses **certainty** with **content**: that my knowledge of mind is more *certain* does not make it richer or prior in *content* | ⚠️ analytic reconstruction | Descartes needs only epistemic priority for the order of the *Meditations* | Fair; "better known" must be read strictly as *more indubitable* |

#### 4. Why the rationalist programme needs this session

⚠️ Without the wax, "Cartesian Method and Certain Knowledge" is a half-answer. The *cogito* gives one certain existential truth; the wax gives the **general epistemology** — that even in the most favourable case for the senses, it is the intellect that does the knowing. That is the claim which makes the reconstruction in Session 5 a *rationalist* reconstruction rather than a lucky escape from doubt.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** The wax argument establishes not that bodies exist but that, if they are known at all, they are known by the intellect and not by the senses — and that self-knowledge is epistemically prior to knowledge of the world.
> **Named evidence.** In Meditation II a piece of wax loses taste, scent, colour, shape, size, hardness, coldness and sound near the fire, yet is judged to be the same wax; imagination cannot supply the indefinitely many shapes that "flexible and changeable" (*flexibile et mutabile*) requires; the same Meditation adds the "hats and coats" case, and its printed sub-title already announces that the mind is better known than the body.
> **Analysis.** Since every member of the sensible catalogue is replaced while the object persists, what is grasped cannot be any of them; and because the range of possible shapes is indefinite, no image can be the vehicle. Only mental scrutiny (*inspectio mentis*) remains, and each such act of judging simultaneously discloses the judging mind.
> **Qualification.** The result is bought at a price the answer should name: identity through total qualitative replacement already presupposes a bearer distinct from its qualities, so Meditation II installs the substance–attribute framework and the essence of body as extension before Meditation VI claims to prove the real distinction — the *Meditations* must therefore be read as one deductive arc, not six independent exercises.

#### Exam link

- **2022 Q4(b), 15 marks** — §2 supplies the exact asymmetry between knowledge of self and knowledge of the world that the question turns on.
- **2019 Q1(d), 10 marks** — the wax must be *excluded* from the proof of external things; saying so explicitly is itself a mark.
- **2025 Q2(a), 20 marks** — the wax is the clearest single demonstration that Descartes' system makes intellect, not sense, the judge.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| "The wax argument proves the external world exists" | It proves nothing about existence; Meditation VI does that, through divine veracity |
| Retelling the melting as a story | The examiner wants numbered steps and a conclusion |
| Omitting imagination from the argument | Step 5 is essential: imagination fails because the possible shapes are indefinite |
| "Better known" means the mind is richer or more fully understood | It means *more indubitable* — epistemic, not content, priority |
| Ignoring the presuppositions | P1 in particular is where a fifteen- or twenty-mark answer separates itself |

#### Revision notes

- Catalogue: taste, scent, colour, shape, size, hardness, coldness, sound.
- All replaced; the same wax is still judged to remain.
- Not sense (all items gone), not imagination (indefinitely many shapes), therefore intellect — *inspectio mentis*.
- Result: extended, flexible, changeable thing; bodies known by understanding; mind *notior*.
- Paired case: "hats and coats" — seeing is judging.
- Presuppositions: bearer metaphysics; sense exhausted by the catalogue; imagination image-bound; perception self-intimating.
- The wax does **not** prove that bodies exist.

#### CLOSING RECALL FLOW — DESCARTES III: THE WAX

```text
CLEAREST CASE OF A BODY: wax from the honeycomb
        | sensible catalogue: taste scent colour shape size hardness cold sound
        v
FIRE DESTROYS EVERY ITEM  ->  YET "the same wax remains"
        |
        +--> not any sensible item (all replaced)
        +--> not imagination (indefinitely many shapes: flexibile et mutabile)
        v
INTELLECT ALONE: inspectio mentis -> extended, flexible, changeable thing
        |
        +--> "hats and coats": what passes for seeing is judging
        v
EVERY JUDGEMENT OF WAX DISCLOSES THE JUDGING MIND -> mind is NOTIOR
        |
        v
ASSUMES: bearer metaphysics | sense = the catalogue | imagination image-bound |
         perception self-intimating
LIMIT: proves HOW body is known, NOT THAT body exists
ANSWER LINE: Meditation II installs extension as body's essence, so Meditation VI's
             dualism is partly presupposed rather than proved
```

### SESSION 5 — DESCARTES IV: THE THREE PROOFS OF GOD, THE CARTESIAN CIRCLE, AND THE EXTERNAL WORLD

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Descartes argues that a perfect God exists, that a perfect being would not systematically deceive, and that this is what finally licenses him to trust his own clear thinking and to believe there is a physical world outside his mind.

**Technical definition:** Descartes offers a causal or "trademark" proof (Meditation III), a conservation or cosmological variant (Meditation III) and an ontological proof (Meditation V); divine veracity is then used to underwrite the general reliability of clear and distinct perception and, through the involuntariness of sensory ideas and an uncorrectable natural inclination, to demonstrate the existence of extended material things (Meditation VI).

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> God is not an appendix to Descartes' epistemology but its load-bearing joint: the thinking self yields one certainty and no more, and it is only a non-deceiving God who converts a rule read off a single case into a general licence to trust reason — which is why the Cartesian Circle is not a peripheral blemish but an objection aimed exactly at the point where the system carries its weight.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **the trademark or causal proof** — objective reality requires a cause with at least as much formal reality
- **the conservation proof** — I cannot sustain myself from moment to moment
- **the ontological proof** — existence belongs to the concept of a supremely perfect being
- **divine veracity** — "God is no deceiver"
- **the Cartesian Circle** — Arnauld, Fourth Objections
- **the memory reply** — present intuition versus remembered demonstration
- **passive faculty of sensory reception; great natural inclination**

**How to use them:** Give the three proofs compactly; state the Circle in one sentence and the reply in one sentence; then run the external-world proof as a numbered argument. Do not let the proofs of God swallow the whole answer.

#### Plain-language visual — the bridge, and the crack in it

```text
    ISLAND 1                 THE BRIDGE                    ISLAND 2
   +-----------+        +------------------------+       +------------------+
   | I EXIST   |        | GOD EXISTS AND IS      |       | MATHEMATICS      |
   | while I   | =====> | NO DECEIVER            | ====> | AND THE EXTERNAL |
   | think     |        | so my clear and        |       | WORLD ARE        |
   +-----------+        | distinct perceptions   |       | RESTORED         |
                        | are generally reliable |       +------------------+
                        +------------------------+
                                  ^
                                  |
              ARNAULD'S QUESTION: what did you use to BUILD the bridge?
              You used clear and distinct perceptions - the very things
              the bridge was supposed to make trustworthy.
```

**In simple words:** Descartes is standing on a small safe island and wants to reach a large one. He builds a bridge out of God. The objection is that the planks of the bridge were cut from the far island he has not yet reached. That is the Circle in one picture — and the reply is that some planks are so solid that you can stand on them while you are looking at them, and God is needed only to certify the ones you have laid down and walked past.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Objective reality | how much reality an idea *represents* |
| Formal reality | how much reality a thing *has* |
| Causal principle | the cause must have at least as much reality as the effect represents |
| Divine veracity | a perfect being will not systematically deceive |
| The Circle | the criterion proves God; God certifies the criterion |

> **One-line memory:** God is the **bridge**, and the Circle is the question of where the **planks** came from.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — three proofs, one objection, one world

```text
  PROOF 1 - TRADEMARK / CAUSAL  (Meditation III)
  +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
  | 1 I have an idea of God: an infinite, perfect being                |
  | 2 The cause of an idea must have at least as much FORMAL reality   |
  |   as the idea has OBJECTIVE reality                                |
  | 3 I, finite and imperfect, cannot be the adequate cause of an      |
  |   idea of an infinite perfect being                                |
  | 4 THEREFORE God exists as its cause - He "stamped" the idea on my  |
  |   mind as a craftsman's mark on his work (hence "trademark")       |
  +-------------------------------------------------------------------+

  PROOF 2 - CONSERVATION / COSMOLOGICAL VARIANT  (Meditation III)
  +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
  | I exist now; nothing in me gives me the power to sustain myself    |
  | from one moment to the next; the cause of my continued existence   |
  | must therefore ultimately be God                                   |
  +-------------------------------------------------------------------+

  PROOF 3 - ONTOLOGICAL  (Meditation V)
  +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
  | 1 The concept of God = the concept of a supremely perfect being    |
  | 2 Existence is a perfection                                        |
  | 3 A supremely perfect being lacking existence would lack a         |
  |   perfection - a contradiction, like a triangle without three      |
  |   angles or a mountain without a valley                            |
  | 4 THEREFORE God necessarily exists                                 |
  |   [Kant will later object that existence is not a real predicate;  |
  |    that is Topic 04 doctrine and belongs here only as one rider]   |
  +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
                                |
                                v
  DIVINE VERACITY: a perfect being is no deceiver
                                |
        +-----------------------+------------------------+
        |                                                |
  GENERAL RELIABILITY OF CLEAR                    THE EXTERNAL WORLD
  AND DISTINCT PERCEPTION                         (Meditation VI)
        |                                                |
  <-- ARNAULD'S CIRCLE STRIKES HERE                1 sensory ideas come
      (Fourth Objections)                            involuntarily -> I have
      criterion -> God -> criterion                   a PASSIVE faculty
        |                                          2 a passive faculty needs
  REPLY: while a clear intuition is being             an ACTIVE cause
  ATTENDED TO it compels assent without a           3 I have a great natural
  guarantee; God is needed for perceptions            INCLINATION to refer
  merely REMEMBERED, and for long chains              them to bodies
  of reasoning                                      4 if no bodies existed and
        |                                             God permitted this
  RESIDUAL: the divine proofs THEMSELVES use          uncorrectable inclination,
  remembered premises and extended inference          God would be a deceiver
                                                    5 THEREFORE material things
                                                      exist as extended realities
```

⚠️ **Caption.** The right-hand column is a five-step argument and should be written as one. Candidates who write "God guarantees the world" have compressed away every mark in the question.

#### 1. The three proofs — what each is for

✅ Descartes does not repeat himself. The three proofs answer different questions:

- The **trademark or causal proof** answers: *where could this idea have come from?* It is the proof that uses the innate idea of God established in Session 3.
- The **conservation proof** answers: *what keeps me in existence?* It converts the *cogito*'s momentary certainty into a demand for a continuing cause.
- The **ontological proof** answers: *what does the concept itself contain?* It requires no premise about me at all, which is why Descartes places it after the criterion has been secured.

⚠️ Naming the division of labour is worth more than reciting all three at length.

#### 2. The Cartesian Circle, stated fairly

✅ **The objection (Arnauld, Fourth Objections).** Descartes uses clear and distinct perception to prove that God exists; he then uses God's veracity to guarantee that clear and distinct perception is reliable. The guarantee therefore presupposes what it is meant to establish.

✅ **The reply.** Descartes distinguishes two situations. While a clear and distinct perception is **actually before the mind**, it compels assent and needs no external certificate. What needs certifying is (i) the perception merely **remembered** as having been clear, and (ii) any **extended chain** of reasoning whose earlier steps are no longer in view. God secures the second class, not the first.

❓ **What still stands.** The proofs of God are not single-flash intuitions; they are arguments with premises, and by the time the conclusion is reached the earlier premises are being remembered rather than attended to. So the reply softens the objection without eliminating it, and honest answers say so.

#### 3. The proof of the external world — the 2019 question in five steps

✅ Meditation VI:

1. Sensory ideas occur **involuntarily**: I cannot decide not to feel heat when near the fire. So I have a **passive faculty** of receiving them.
2. A passive faculty requires an **active cause** which contains that reality formally or eminently.
3. The candidates are: myself, God directly, or bodies. It is not myself, because the ideas arrive without my willing them.
4. I have a **great natural inclination** to refer them to bodies, and no faculty by which God could have given me the means to correct it. If no bodies existed, God would be permitting an uncorrectable systematic error — that is, deceiving.
5. ∴ Material things exist as **extended** realities.

⚠️ **What the proof secures and what it does not.** It secures the existence of body and the reality of extension far more strongly than the literal accuracy of any particular colour, size, distance or sensible quality. Descartes insists that the intellect must correct sensory error; the senses are reliable for what is useful to the mind–body union, not for metaphysics.

❌ **The wax argument does not do this work.** The wax shows how bodies would be known; it says nothing about whether any exist.

#### 4. God's place in the architecture

| What God supplies | Where it is used | If God fails |
|---|---|---|
| General reliability of clear and distinct perception | everywhere after Meditation III | nothing beyond the momentary *cogito* survives |
| Trustworthiness of memory and long proofs | mathematics; all systematic science | the system contracts to isolated intuitions |
| The non-deceiving guarantee behind natural inclination | the external world | the world remains unproved |
| Continuous conservation of finite substances | the substance metaphysics of Session 6 | "created substance" loses its ground |

⚠️ This table is the reason the standard verdict runs: *the* Meditations *stand or fall on Meditation III, not Meditation II.*

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** Descartes proves the existence of things other than himself and God by a causal argument that is licensed entirely by divine veracity.
