### SESSION 10 — LEIBNIZ I: MONADS, PERCEPTION AND APPETITION, THE HIERARCHY, AND THE IDENTITY OF INDISCERNIBLES

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Leibniz agrees with Spinoza that Descartes' extended substance cannot be ultimate, but refuses to let individuals disappear into one thing — so he makes the world out of infinitely many partless, soul-like units, each of which mirrors the whole universe from its own point of view.

**Technical definition:** A monad (*monas*) is a simple, unextended, indestructible substance without parts, endowed with perception (*perceptio*) — an internal representation of the manifold in the unity — and appetition (*appetitus*) — the internal principle by which it passes from one perception to the next; monads are windowless, differentiated by the identity of indiscernibles, arranged in a hierarchy, and extended bodies are well-founded phenomena (*phaenomena bene fundata*) grounded in ordered aggregates of them.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Leibniz's monads are not a mystical addition to metaphysics but the conclusion of a short and severe argument: whatever is extended is divisible, whatever is divisible is composite, and a composite presupposes the simples of which it is composed — so if there are aggregates at all, there must be partless units, and since these can have no shape or size, their only available inner states are perception and striving.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **monad (*monas*)** — simple, partless, unextended, indestructible
- **"monads have no windows"** (*les monades n'ont point de fenêtres*, *Monadology* §7)
- **perception (*perceptio*) and appetition (*appetitus*)**
- **minute perceptions (*petites perceptions*)** and **apperception**
- **bare monads, animal souls and rational spirits**
- **dominant monad and organic aggregate**
- **identity of indiscernibles**
- **well-founded phenomena (*phaenomena bene fundata*)**

**How to use them:** Give the argument to simples first; then perception and appetition as the only inner states available; then the hierarchy; then indiscernibles as the guarantee of individuality.

#### Plain-language visual — why the world cannot be made of stuff

```text
   TAKE ANY EXTENDED THING
              |
     it has left and right halves -> IT IS DIVISIBLE
              |
     a divisible thing is a COMPOSITE - a collection
              |
     a collection is made of members; the members, if extended, are
     themselves collections; and so on WITHOUT END
              |
     BUT: an aggregate must be an aggregate OF something.
     A heap of heaps of heaps, with no grains anywhere, is no heap.
              |
              v
     THEREFORE there must be SIMPLES: partless units
              |
     A partless unit has no shape, no size, no position
     (all of those require parts)
              |
     SO WHAT INNER STATES CAN IT HAVE AT ALL?
              |
     Only states of REPRESENTING (perception) and of
     PASSING FROM ONE REPRESENTATION TO THE NEXT (appetition)
              |
              v
                   THIS IS A MONAD
```

**In simple words:** Leibniz's monads look strange until you see that they are forced. Once you agree that a pile must be a pile *of* something, and that a partless something can have no geometry, the only properties left for it are inner ones — and "inner state" plus "change of inner state" just is perception plus appetition.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Simple | having no parts; therefore indivisible and naturally indestructible |
| Perception | the representation of many in one; not necessarily conscious |
| Appetition | the internal tendency carrying one perception into the next |
| Windowless | nothing enters or leaves; all change is internal |
| Well-founded phenomenon | not an illusion, but not an ultimate substance either |

> **One-line memory:** monads have no **windows** — and therefore no **imports** and no **exports**.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — the monadic hierarchy and the two doctrines that individuate it

```text
                         GOD
        the unique UNCREATED necessary simple substance;
        source of all monads; complete and infinite intelligence
        [CAUTION: "the supreme Monad" is common shorthand, but God
         is NOT merely the top member of the created series]
                          |
        =========== CREATED MONADS: A HIERARCHY ===========
                          |
   +----------------------+----------------------+
   |                      |                      |
 RATIONAL SPIRITS     ANIMAL SOULS          BARE MONADS
 (minds)              (souls proper)        (bare entelechies)
 apperception -       memory + distinct     only obscure and
 conscious reflexive  feeling; perception   confused MINUTE
 awareness; grasp     raised above the      PERCEPTIONS
 NECESSARY TRUTHS;    bare level;           (petites perceptions);
 moral agency;        consecutive           no apperception;
 "images of the       sequences of          a kind of dreamless
 Deity"               images                stupor
   |                      |                      |
   +----------------------+----------------------+
                          |
        EVERY monad, at every level, has PERCEPTION and APPETITION,
        and each represents the WHOLE universe from its own point of
        view - "a perpetual living mirror of the universe"
                          |
   TWO DOCTRINES THAT MAKE THIS WORK
   +-------------------------------------------------------------+
   | 1  NO WINDOWS  (Monadology Sec.7)                            |
   |    "Les Monades n'ont point de fenetres" - no causal influx  |
   |    from outside; every change unfolds from the monad's own   |
   |    internal principle                                        |
   |                                                              |
   | 2  IDENTITY OF INDISCERNIBLES                                |
   |    No two monads are exactly alike. If two were, God would   |
   |    have had NO SUFFICIENT REASON to create both rather than  |
   |    one - so perfect duplicates are impossible                |
   |    -> individuality is GUARANTEED, not merely permitted      |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------+
                          |
   AND WHAT ABOUT BODIES?
   +-------------------------------------------------------------+
   | An organism is coordinated by a DOMINANT MONAD together with |
   | an organic aggregate of subordinate monads - NOT by exchanged|
   | signals or causal control                                    |
   |                                                              |
   | Extended bodies are WELL-FOUNDED PHENOMENA (phaenomena bene  |
   | fundata): grounded in ordered monadic aggregates, therefore  |
   | neither ultimate substances NOR arbitrary private illusions  |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------+
```

⚠️ **Caption.** The bottom box is the one candidates omit, and it is the one that stops "monads" sounding like idealism. A well-founded phenomenon is *objective* — it has a real ground — without being *ultimate*.

#### 1. Why extension cannot be the essence of substance

✅ Leibniz's argument against Descartes is short:

1. Extension is essentially **divisible**.
2. Whatever is divisible is a **composite**, an aggregate of parts.
3. An aggregate derives its reality from the reality of its constituents.
4. ∴ Extended things cannot be ultimate; there must be **simple** substances, and these cannot themselves be extended.

⚠️ Note that this is an attack on Spinoza too. If extension is not a possible essence of substance, then Extension cannot be an attribute expressing the essence of the one substance in the way Spinoza needs.

#### 2. Perception and appetition — the only inner states available

✅ **Perception** in Leibniz's technical sense is "the representation of the manifold in the simple" — the monad's internal representation of the entire universe from its own point of view. It does **not** imply consciousness.

✅ **Appetition** is the internal principle producing change: the tendency by which a monad passes from one perception to the next. It is what makes the monad an active, self-unfolding thing rather than a static point.

✅ **Minute perceptions (*petites perceptions*)** are perceptions below the threshold of reflective awareness. Leibniz's own illustration is the roar of the sea: we hear the whole roar, so we must in some way perceive each wave, though we notice none individually.

✅ **Apperception** is conscious, reflexive awareness of perception. ⚠️ Crucially, apperception must **not** be attributed to every monad — this is the single most common error in monadology answers. Every monad perceives; only some apperceive.

#### 3. The hierarchy

| Level | Perception | Distinctive capacity |
|---|---|---|
| **Bare monads (bare entelechies)** | obscure, confused, minute perceptions only | no memory, no apperception; a dreamless stupor |
| **Animal souls** | perception raised to distinct feeling | **memory**, which produces a "consecutiveness" resembling reason |
| **Rational spirits (minds)** | distinct perception plus reflexive awareness | **apperception**; knowledge of **necessary and eternal truths**; science and morality; capable of entering into a moral community with God |

⚠️ **God's place.** God is the unique **uncreated** necessary simple substance and the source of all created monads. Calling God "the supreme Monad" is common shorthand, but it is a shorthand — God is not merely the highest member of the created series.

#### 4. "Monads have no windows"

✅ *Monadology* §7, as it is usually rendered: **"Monads have no windows through which anything could come in or go out."** No causal influx enters a monad; nothing is transferred between them. All change arises internally, from the monad's own appetition, unfolding what its nature already contains.

⚠️ Translation discipline: the French is *les Monades n'ont point de fenêtres*. "No windows" is a standard rendering, not a literal English original, so write "as it is usually rendered".

⚠️ The immediate consequence is that the mind–body problem cannot be solved by *any* form of interaction — which is what Session 11's pre-established harmony exists to handle.

#### 5. The identity of indiscernibles

✅ **No two monads are exactly alike.** If two substances were qualitatively identical, God would have had no sufficient reason to create both rather than one; and since nothing happens without a sufficient reason, perfect duplicates are impossible.

✅ Two things follow that matter for the exam:

- Individuality is **guaranteed**, not merely permitted. This is precisely what Spinoza's system cannot deliver, and it is Leibniz's principal motive.
- Each monad differs by the *degree of distinctness* of its perceptions and by its unique point of view on the same universe — which is why there is one world, represented infinitely many times.

#### 6. Bodies as well-founded phenomena

✅ An organism is coordinated by a **dominant monad** together with an organic aggregate of subordinate monads. This is coordination, not causal control through exchanged signals.

✅ Extended bodies are **well-founded phenomena (*phaenomena bene fundata*)**: they are grounded in ordered monadic aggregates. ⚠️ They are therefore *not* ultimate substances, but neither are they arbitrary private illusions — the phrase "well-founded" is doing real work and must not be dropped.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** Leibniz's monadology is generated by a refusal of extended substance rather than by any independent enthusiasm for souls, and its point is to secure individuality that Spinoza's monism cannot.
> **Named evidence.** *Monadology* §§1–3 argue that composites presuppose simples and that simples, being partless, can have neither shape nor divisibility; §7 states that monads have no windows through which anything could enter or leave; §§14–15 introduce perception and appetition; §§19–29 grade bare monads, animal souls with memory and rational spirits capable of necessary truths; the identity of indiscernibles is defended in the correspondence with Clarke on the ground that God would have no sufficient reason to create indistinguishable duplicates.
> **Analysis.** Each doctrine is forced by the preceding one: partlessness removes every geometrical property, so only representational states remain; windowlessness follows because nothing partless can receive an influx; and since qualitative duplication would leave God's choice unmotivated, the units must be individuated intrinsically rather than by position.
> **Qualification.** The gain is real but expensive. Individuality is secured, yet ordinary bodies are demoted to well-founded phenomena and the correspondence between a mind's perceptions and its body's states can no longer be explained by any interaction — which is exactly the debt pre-established harmony is introduced to discharge, and which critics regard as buying agreement in advance rather than explaining it.

#### Exam link

- **2020 Q1(b), 10 marks** — "Leibniz's conception of monads" is §1 to §5, and the bearing on determinism and freedom is completed in Session 11.
- **2018 Q2(a), 20 marks** — Leibniz is the third term: simplicity added to independence produces infinitely many substances.
- **2024 Q3(c), 15 marks** — windowlessness is why Leibniz cannot answer the mind–body question by interaction, and therefore why he needs harmony.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| "Monads interact with one another" | They are windowless; there is no causal influx at all |
| "Every monad is conscious" | Every monad **perceives**; only some **apperceive** |
| Treating monads as physical atoms | Atoms are extended and divisible in principle; monads are partless and non-spatial |
| Saying bodies are illusions | They are *well-founded* phenomena, grounded in ordered aggregates |
| Calling God "the supreme Monad" without qualification | God is uncreated and necessary, not the top of the created series |
| Ignoring minute perceptions | Without them, the roar of the sea and unconscious mental life cannot be explained |

#### Revision notes

- Argument to simples: extension → divisible → composite → aggregates need simples.
- Monad: simple, unextended, indestructible; states = perception + appetition.
- *Petites perceptions* below awareness; apperception only in rational spirits.
- Hierarchy: bare entelechies → animal souls (memory) → rational spirits (necessary truths).
- *Monadology* §7: "monads have no windows", as usually rendered.
- Identity of indiscernibles: no sufficient reason for duplicates → individuality guaranteed.
- Dominant monad + organic aggregate; bodies = well-founded phenomena.

#### CLOSING RECALL FLOW — LEIBNIZ I: MONADS

```text
EXTENSION IS DIVISIBLE -> COMPOSITE -> AGGREGATES PRESUPPOSE SIMPLES
        |
        v
SIMPLE SUBSTANCE = MONAD: partless, unextended, indestructible
        | a partless thing can have no shape, size or position
        v
ONLY INNER STATES REMAIN: PERCEPTION (many represented in one) and
APPETITION (the internal passage from perception to perception)
        |
        +--> petites perceptions below awareness (the roar of the sea)
        +--> APPERCEPTION only in rational spirits - NOT in every monad
        v
HIERARCHY: bare entelechies -> animal souls (memory) -> rational spirits
           (apperception, necessary truths, moral community with God)
GOD: the unique UNCREATED necessary simple substance, not the top of the series
        |
        v
NO WINDOWS (Monadology Sec.7): no influx in or out; all change is internal
IDENTITY OF INDISCERNIBLES: duplicates would leave God without a sufficient
reason -> individuality is GUARANTEED
        |
        v
DOMINANT MONAD + ORGANIC AGGREGATE -> BODIES ARE WELL-FOUNDED PHENOMENA
(grounded, therefore not illusions; not ultimate, therefore not substances)
        |
        v
CONSEQUENCE: interaction is impossible in principle -> harmony must do the work
TRAP: monads are not atoms | perception is not consciousness
ANSWER LINE: monads are what is left when divisibility and influx are both denied
```

### SESSION 11 — LEIBNIZ II: SUFFICIENT REASON, TWO KINDS OF TRUTH, POSSIBLE WORLDS, PRE-ESTABLISHED HARMONY AND FREEDOM

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Since nothing happens without a reason, there must be a being outside the chain of contingent things who explains it; that being chose this world from infinitely many possible ones because it is the best; and he set every monad going so that they all keep perfect time with each other without ever touching.

**Technical definition:** The Principle of Sufficient Reason holds that nothing is without a reason why it is so rather than otherwise; truths of reason are necessary and their opposite implies a contradiction, while truths of fact are contingent and their complete analysis is infinite; God, as the necessary being demanded by the Principle, actualises the best of all compossible worlds; and pre-established harmony coordinates the internally unfolding states of all monads, yielding a compatibilist freedom composed of spontaneity, intelligence and contingency.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Pre-established harmony denies causal interaction but not agency: a monad's states unfold from its own internal principle, so the very doctrine that removes external influence is what guarantees that action is *self*-expression — which is why Leibniz can claim, without contradiction, that a world in which nothing pushes anything is a world in which freedom is safest.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **Principle of Sufficient Reason (PSR)** — nothing without a reason
- **truths of reason and truths of fact**
- **complete individual concept**
- **possible worlds; compossibility; the best of all possible worlds** (*le meilleur des mondes possibles*)
- **pre-established harmony; the two synchronised clocks**
- **spontaneity, intelligence and contingency**
- **"incline without necessitating"** (*incliner sans nécessiter*)
- **hypothetical or moral necessity versus absolute or logical necessity**

**How to use them:** PSR first, because it generates God, indiscernibles and the two kinds of truth alike; then harmony; then the three conditions of freedom; then the necessity distinction, which is where the marks are.

#### Plain-language visual — two clocks that were never connected

```text
     CLOCK A  (the mind-monad)              CLOCK B  (the body's monads)
   +---------------------------+          +---------------------------+
   | 10:00  decides to speak   |          | 10:00  lips begin to move |
   | 10:01  intends the word   |   ||     | 10:01  tongue positions   |
   | 10:02  hears itself speak |   ||     | 10:02  air is disturbed   |
   +---------------------------+          +---------------------------+
                  \\                              //
                   \\  NO ROD, NO WIRE, NO PUSH  //
                    \\                          //
                     ============================
                                 |
              THEY AGREE BECAUSE THEY WERE SET, AT THE OUTSET,
              BY A CLOCKMAKER OF PERFECT SKILL
                                 |
   THE THREE OPTIONS LEIBNIZ HIMSELF LISTS
   1 INFLUENCE   - a rod between them        = Descartes' interaction
   2 ASSISTANCE  - a keeper adjusting them   = occasionalism
                   at every moment
   3 HARMONY     - perfect construction at   = LEIBNIZ
                   the start
```

**In simple words:** the second option — a keeper constantly correcting the clocks — is occasionalism, and Leibniz rejects it as unworthy of a perfect craftsman: a clockmaker who must keep intervening is a poor clockmaker. Naming all three options and rejecting two is what turns a picture into an argument.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| PSR | nothing is so rather than otherwise without a reason |
| Truth of reason | opposite implies a contradiction; necessary |
| Truth of fact | opposite is conceivable; contingent, though certain |
| Compossible | able to exist together in one world |
| Incline without necessitating | reasons make a choice certain, not logically compelled |

> **One-line memory:** harmony is set **once, at the start** — not maintained by repeated divine repairs.

⚠️ **Caption.** The two-line necessity box is the highest-value content in the whole Leibniz half of this topic. Both 2018 Q1(b) and 2020 Q1(b) are won or lost there.

#### 1. The Principle of Sufficient Reason, and the proof of God

✅ **PSR:** nothing is without a reason why it is so rather than otherwise.

✅ **The argument to God** is a cosmological argument in PSR form:

1. Contingent things exist, and each has its reason in a prior contingent thing.
2. The whole *series* of contingent things is itself contingent, and so requires a sufficient reason.
3. That reason cannot lie within the series, since every member is contingent.
4. ∴ There must be a **necessary being** outside the series, whose reason for existing lies in its own nature. That is God.

✅ Leibniz also accepts an **ontological** route, but characteristically adds a repair: one must first show that the concept of a supremely perfect being is **possible** — that its perfections are compatible — before inferring existence from it.

#### 2. Truths of reason and truths of fact

| | **Truths of reason** | **Truths of fact** |
|---|---|---|
| Example | "a triangle has three sides"; the truths of mathematics and logic | "Caesar crossed the Rubicon"; "this world contains India" |
| Opposite | implies a **contradiction** | is **conceivable**; implies no contradiction |
| Modality | **necessary** | **contingent**, though certain |
| Governed by | the principle of contradiction | the Principle of Sufficient Reason |
| Analysis | **finite**: can in principle be reduced to identities | **infinite**: completed only by God |

⚠️ This distinction is Leibniz's own answer to the charge that his complete-concept doctrine makes everything necessary, and it is the pivot of the freedom answer in §5.

#### 3. Possible worlds, compossibility and the best world

✅ God's understanding contains infinitely many **possible** individuals and worlds. Not all possibles are **compossible** — capable of existing together — so possible worlds are maximal compossible sets.

✅ God, being perfectly good and wise, actualises the world with the **greatest perfection**. Leibniz's own gloss on "best" is **the greatest variety of phenomena governed by the simplest laws** — maximum richness with minimum means.

❌ "Best" does **not** mean maximum human comfort or the absence of suffering. This is the standard misreading, and it is the misreading Voltaire's *Candide* (1759) satirises.

⚠️ **Reception versus doctrine.** *Candide* is a literary reaction, not a philosophical refutation, and citing it as though it stated Leibniz's position is a clear error. Cite it, correctly labelled, as the reason the phrase acquired its ironic sense.

✅ **Theodicy in one line, as far as this topic owns it:** evil is permitted because a world containing it is, overall, the most perfect compossible whole; the perfection is a property of the *whole system*, not of each part taken alone.

#### 4. Pre-established harmony

✅ Since monads are windowless, mind and body do not really interact. God arranged all monads **at creation** so that the perceptions of the mind-monad and the states of the body's monads correspond perfectly.

✅ **The two-clocks image.** Two perfectly made clocks keep the same time without any connection between them. Leibniz explicitly contrasts three possibilities — **influence** (a rod between them: Descartes), **assistance** (a keeper constantly adjusting them: occasionalism), and **harmony** (perfect construction at the outset: his own view) — and rejects the first as unintelligible and the second as unworthy of a perfect craftsman.

⚠️ **The standing objection:** harmony is *ad hoc* and unfalsifiable; it preserves the *appearance* of interaction while denying its reality, and it explains any correspondence whatever equally well. **Leibniz's reply:** it is the only account that avoids both unintelligible cross-substance causation and the loss of a genuine plurality of substances; and unlike occasionalism it does not require continuous miracles.

#### 5. Freedom — the answer to the 2018 and 2020 questions

✅ Leibniz rejects **both** Spinozan necessitarianism **and** Cartesian libertarian indifference. His compatibilism requires three conditions **together**:

1. **Spontaneity.** The action springs from the agent's own nature; there is no external compulsion. Windowlessness *guarantees* this — nothing outside the monad can push it.
2. **Intelligence.** The agent deliberates and acts for perceived reasons, not by blind impact. This is what distinguishes a rational spirit from a bare monad.
3. **Contingency.** The actual world is one of infinitely many possible worlds; the opposite of the act implies no contradiction and is realised in some other possible world.

✅ **"Reasons incline without necessitating" (*incliner sans nécessiter*).** The strongest motive prevails and the outcome is certain, but the determination is **moral**, not **logical**: it remains conceivable that the agent act otherwise, in the sense that no contradiction follows from supposing it.

❓ **The residual difficulty, which must be stated.** If every predicate of an individual is already contained in its complete concept, and God has actualised this world, then *this* agent could not now act otherwise. The availability of alternatives in other possible worlds may not satisfy a libertarian who demands that *this* agent, in *these* circumstances, could have done otherwise. Leibniz's contingency can therefore look merely epistemic from the creature's standpoint.

⚠️ **The examiner-safe verdict:** pre-established harmony leaves a defensible place for responsible self-expression, but not for libertarian alternative possibilities. It yields coherent compatibilism, not indeterministic freedom.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** Pre-established harmony makes room for freedom rather than abolishing it, because it converts every action into an unfolding of the agent's own nature.
> **Named evidence.** *Monadology* §7 denies all causal influx, so a monad's states arise from its internal principle of appetition alone; the *Discourse on Metaphysics* holds that every predicate ever true of an individual is contained in its complete concept; the *Theodicy* and the correspondence with Clarke supply the possible-worlds framework and the formula that reasons incline without necessitating (*incliner sans nécessiter*).
> **Analysis.** Freedom then requires three conditions jointly — spontaneity, which windowlessness itself guarantees; intelligence, which distinguishes rational spirits from bare monads; and contingency, which survives because "Caesar crosses the Rubicon" is certain in the world God chose but implies no contradiction and is denied in other possible worlds. The determination is therefore moral rather than logical.
> **Qualification.** The concession worth making is precise: since the complete concept of the actual agent is fixed and this world is the one actualised, alternatives are available across worlds rather than within this one, so a libertarian who insists that *this* agent could now have done otherwise will regard Leibnizian contingency as epistemic rather than real.

#### Exam link

- **2018 Q1(b), 10 marks** — "any place for freedom … pre-established harmony" is §4 and §5 exactly.
- **2020 Q1(b), 10 marks** — the same material, but the directive "with your own comments" requires an explicit signposted personal verdict in the final quarter.
- **2024 Q3(c), 15 marks** — Leibniz is the defended winner, on the compatibilist criterion.
- **2018 Q2(a), 20 marks** — §1 supplies why Leibniz's method differs: PSR, not doubt and not geometrical demonstration.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| Calling Leibniz a libertarian | He is a compatibilist; he rejects indifference explicitly |
| Calling Leibniz a fatalist or necessitarian | He distinguishes hypothetical from absolute necessity precisely to avoid this |
| Treating harmony as continuous divine intervention | That is **occasionalism**, which Leibniz rejects; harmony is set once at creation |
| Reading "best of all possible worlds" as maximum comfort | It means greatest variety under simplest laws |
| Citing *Candide* as Leibniz's view | It is Voltaire's 1759 satire — reception, not doctrine |
| Giving only spontaneity as Leibniz's account of freedom | All three conditions are required together |

#### Revision notes

- PSR: nothing without a reason; grounds God, indiscernibles and truths of fact.
- Cosmological route: contingent series needs a necessary being outside it.
- Truths of reason: opposite contradictory, finite analysis. Truths of fact: opposite conceivable, infinite analysis, God alone completes it.
- Complete individual concept contains every predicate.
- Possible worlds; compossibility; best = greatest variety under simplest laws.
- Harmony: influence / assistance / harmony — the third is Leibniz's; set once at creation; two clocks.
- Freedom = spontaneity + intelligence + contingency; "incline without necessitating".
- Hypothetical or moral necessity ≠ absolute or logical necessity.

```text
        |

        +--> TRUTHS OF REASON: opposite contradictory | finite analysis
        +--> TRUTHS OF FACT: opposite conceivable | INFINITE analysis, God alone
        +--> IDENTITY OF INDISCERNIBLES (Session 10)
        +--> BEST OF ALL POSSIBLE WORLDS: maximum variety, simplest laws
             [Candide 1759 = reception, NOT doctrine]

        |
        v
IS EVERYTHING THEREFORE NECESSARY?  NO:
  ABSOLUTE / LOGICAL necessity -> only truths of reason
  HYPOTHETICAL / MORAL necessity -> certain given God's choice, opposite still
                                    conceivable in another possible world
        |
        v
PRE-ESTABLISHED HARMONY: three options - INFLUENCE (Descartes) | ASSISTANCE
(occasionalism) | HARMONY (Leibniz, set once at creation) -> TWO CLOCKS
        |
        v
FREEDOM = SPONTANEITY (windowlessness guarantees it)
        + INTELLIGENCE (acts for perceived reasons)
        + CONTINGENCY (other possible worlds)
   reasons INCLINE WITHOUT NECESSITATING (incliner sans necessiter)
        |
        v
CONCEDE: alternatives lie ACROSS worlds, not WITHIN this one
VERDICT: coherent compatibilism, not indeterministic freedom
TRAP: not occasionalism | not fatalism | not libertarianism | Candide is satire
ANSWER LINE: the doctrine that removes external influence is the same doctrine
             that makes action self-expression
```

### SESSION 12 — COMPARISON, CRITICISMS AND REPLIES, ANSWER ARCHITECTURE, DIRECTIVES AND PROVENANCE

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** This session puts the three systems side by side on the same questions, collects the standard objections and the best replies, and then turns all of it into a method for writing the answer the examiner actually asked for.

**Technical definition:** A comparative and metacognitive session: matched-axis comparison of substance, God, mind–body and freedom; a criticism–reply–residual ledger for each thinker; directive decoding for the command words used in UPSC Philosophy Paper I; graded verdicts at 10, 15 and 20 marks; and translation, quotation and provenance discipline.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The three rationalists are best read not as three answers to one question but as three verdicts on whether the question was well formed: Descartes accepts the mind–body problem and cannot answer it, Spinoza dissolves it by denying the two-substance premise, and Leibniz dissolves it by denying causation between substances altogether — and the fact that no single criterion selects among them is itself the strongest evidence that what Descartes bequeathed was a pseudo-problem rather than a problem.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **matched axes** — compare on the *same* question, or the comparison is decorative
- **state the criterion before ranking**
- **explanatory economy versus fidelity to experience versus systematic consistency**
- **directive fidelity** — discuss, critically discuss, comment, explain, substantiate
- **structural citation** — *Ethics* I, Def. 7; II, P7; Meditation III; *Monadology* §7
- **provenance caution** — Letter 50; Ryle's coinage; *Candide*

**How to use them:** In any comparative question, name the axis, run all three, state the criterion, deliver a ranked verdict. Never present three descriptions and stop.

#### Plain-language visual — the same four questions, asked three times

```text
                     DESCARTES        SPINOZA           LEIBNIZ
   HOW MANY          three            one               infinitely many
   SUBSTANCES?       (analogically)   (strictly)        (simple, active)

   WHAT IS GOD       GUARANTEES       IS the system     SELECTS and
   FOR?              knowledge        (immanent cause)  harmonises

   MIND AND BODY?    INTERACT         are ONE THING     NEVER interact
                     (pineal gland)   twice expressed   (harmony)

   WHAT IS           the will can     acting from       spontaneity +
   FREEDOM?          affirm, deny     one's own         intelligence +
                     or withhold      understood nature contingency

   ------------------------------------------------------------------
   READ DOWN a column = one system.
   READ ACROSS a row  = one examination question.
   The examiner almost always asks you to read ACROSS.
```

**In simple words:** most weak scripts read down the columns — a paragraph on Descartes, a paragraph on Spinoza, a paragraph on Leibniz. Strong scripts read across the rows and then say which answer is better *by a stated standard*. That single habit is worth more than any additional fact.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Matched axis | all three answer the *same* question in the same terms |
| Criterion | the standard by which you will rank them |
| Verdict | a defended position, not a summary |
| Residual | what survives even after the best reply |
| Provenance | who actually said the words you are quoting |

> **One-line memory:** a comparison without a **criterion** is a **list**.

```text
  |          | unfalsifiable       | route avoiding both| agreement in     |
  |          |                     | unintelligible     | advance          |
  |          |                     | influx and monism  |                  |
  | Leibniz  | "best of all        | "best" = maximum   | the criterion of |
  |          | possible worlds" -> | variety under      | perfection is    |
  |          | Voltaire's Candide  | simplest laws, not | not independently|
  |          |                     | maximum comfort    | testable         |
  | Leibniz  | freedom is illusory | contingency is     | alternatives are |
  |          | if every state is   | preserved: the     | across worlds,   |
  |          | in the complete     | opposite implies no| not within this  |
  |          | concept             | contradiction      | one              |
  +----------+---------------------+--------------------+------------------+
```

⚠️ **Caption.** The residual column is what separates a good answer from a top answer. Reporting a criticism and a reply is exposition; saying what survives the reply is evaluation.

#### 1. Ranking, and why the criterion must come first

⚠️ Every comparative question in this topic is decided by the criterion you announce:

| If freedom means … | Then the winner is … | And the price is … |
|---|---|---|
| genuine alternative possibilities | **Descartes** | an interaction he cannot make intelligible |
| acting from an adequate understanding of one's own nature | **Spinoza** | libertarian free will disappears entirely |
| determined yet responsible agency | **Leibniz** | correspondence is bought from God in advance |

✅ The same discipline applies to the mind–body question: judged by **explanatory economy** Spinoza wins; judged by **fidelity to experience** Descartes wins; judged by **systematic consistency** Leibniz wins.

⚠️ For the 2024 comparative question, the examiner-safe ruling is **Leibniz**, *provided the criterion is stated*: his is the only account that keeps both the determination the metaphysics requires and the responsibility ethics requires. A bare assertion that "Leibniz preserves freedom", without hypothetical necessity, internal spontaneity and possible worlds, earns very little.

#### 2. Directive decoder — what each command word obliges you to do

| Directive, as printed | What it demands | Structural obligation here | Fatal error |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Discuss** | exposition **and** assessment, both substantial | doctrine reconstructed as numbered steps, then a weighing paragraph; roughly 60:40 | pure exposition with no evaluative sentence |
| **Critically discuss / examine** | assessment must **dominate** | reconstruct in three or four lines, then spend the bulk on objection → reply → adjudication; a verdict is compulsory | listing criticisms without ruling between them |
| **Comment** (2025 Q2b) | short, pointed, thesis-first | open by stating what the sentence means **inside Spinoza's own system**; then one consequence for God, one for finite modes, one for knowledge; then a one-line judgement | treating "comment" as an invitation to a general essay on Spinoza |
| **Explain** (2019 Q2c; 2020 Q4b) | make the doctrine **intelligible**, showing why it follows | foreground the derivation — from Definition 7, P16, P29 — not from summary | restating the quotation in other words |
| **Substantiate your view** (2022 Q2c) | take a side and defend it with textual grounds | declare "yes, it entails pantheism, in the following precise sense…", then defend against the acosmism reading | sitting on the fence |
| **Discuss with your own comments** (2020 Q1b) | an explicit first-person evaluative paragraph is being marked | reserve the final quarter for a signposted personal adjudication | omitting the personal verdict |
| **Whose account is compatible with …? Critically discuss** (2024 Q3c) | a comparative **ranking** with a defended winner | rank all three against a stated criterion of freedom, then defend the ranking | describing all three neutrally |
| **What is the reason for the difference …?** (2018 Q2a) | **explanatory**, not descriptive | give the *mechanism*: one inherited definition, three different applications of the independence criterion | tabulating three views without explaining the divergence |

#### 3. Graded verdict bank — executable closing positions

⚠️ Each of these is a **defensible position**, not a summary. Use or compress.

| Sub-topic | 10-mark verdict | 15-mark verdict, with the concession | 20-mark verdict: thesis + mechanism + cost |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Cartesian method** | The method of doubt is not scepticism but scepticism's instrument: it is used to destroy itself, and it succeeds only because the *cogito* is performatively, not inferentially, certain. | … Its success is local: the *cogito* secures the existence of a thinker but not the substantiality of the thinker, and Hume and Kant both exploit exactly this gap. | Descartes' method delivers a genuine indubitable, but the reconstruction built on it is only as strong as the divine guarantee — so the *Meditations* stand or fall on Meditation III, not Meditation II, and the Circle is the system's load-bearing joint rather than a peripheral blemish. |
| **The wax argument** | The wax proves not that bodies exist but that, if known, they are known by intellect — and that self-knowledge is epistemically prior. | … It succeeds against naive empiricism because no sensory item registers *identity through change*; it fails to establish apriority, since the empiricist can call extension an abstraction. | Meditation II already installs extension as body's essence, so Meditation VI's dualism is partly presupposed rather than proved; the wax is the hinge on which Cartesian dualism silently turns. |
| **Substance across the trio** | The three differ not because they disagree about the *definition* of substance but because they differ over how strictly to apply it. | … Descartes applies it loosely, admitting dependent created substances; Spinoza strictly, so that only the wholly independent qualifies; Leibniz adds simplicity, so that only the partless qualifies. | The trio is one argument unfolding: Spinoza's monism is the consistent Descartes and Leibniz's pluralism is the consistent rejection of extension as an essence — so "substance" is not a discovery but a criterion, and the metaphysics you get is the criterion you enforce. |
| **God** | For Descartes God is a guarantee, for Spinoza God is the system, for Leibniz God is a selector. | … Only Spinoza's God does no explanatory work *outside* the system, which is why his alone does not stand or fall with the ontological proof. | Kant's objection that existence is not a real predicate bears directly on Descartes' and Leibniz's God but leaves Spinoza's largely untouched, because Spinoza does not infer God's existence from a concept of perfection — he identifies God with substance itself. |
| **Mind and body** | The three solutions are not three answers to one question but three verdicts on whether the question is well formed. | … Descartes accepts the question and cannot answer it; Spinoza dissolves it by denying the two-substance premise; Leibniz dissolves it by denying causation between substances. | Judged by explanatory economy Spinoza wins, by fidelity to experience Descartes wins, by systematic consistency Leibniz wins — and that no single criterion selects one answer is itself the strongest evidence that Descartes bequeathed a pseudo-problem. |
| **Freedom and determinism** | Spinoza does not abolish freedom, he relocates it — from the will's indifference to the intellect's adequacy. | … Whether that is freedom or determinism renamed depends on whether "could have done otherwise" is essential to freedom; Spinoza denies it, and denies it consistently. | Against a stated criterion: if freedom requires alternative possibilities only Descartes qualifies, at the price of an unintelligible interaction; if it requires self-determination Spinoza qualifies best; if it requires reconciling determination with responsibility, Leibniz's spontaneity, intelligence and contingency is the most workable — and that is the examiner-safe ruling. |
| **The three kinds of knowledge** | Spinoza's three kinds are three ontological positions of the knower, not three degrees of confidence. | … This makes epistemology and ethics one subject, his most original move; but because intuitive knowledge is self-certifying (*verum index sui*), it is also his most vulnerable. | The tripartite scheme is what converts necessitarianism from a fatalism into an ethics: because knowing adequately *is* acting from one's own nature, the discovery that all is necessary is itself the act by which one becomes free — at the price that no external criterion distinguishes the third kind from confident imagination. |

#### 4. Translation, quotation and provenance discipline

✅ **Rule of this topic:** quote verbatim only where a standard English edition is being followed and named; elsewhere paraphrase and mark it as paraphrase. Never present a reconstructed sentence in quotation marks.

| Text | Standard editions | Safe practice |
|---|---|---|
| Descartes, *Meditations on First Philosophy* (Latin 1641; French 1647) | Cottingham–Stoothoff–Murdoch, *The Philosophical Writings of Descartes* (CUP, 1984) — the scholarly default; the older Haldane–Ross (1911) is still common in Indian editions | Wording differs between editions: *inspectio mentis* is "purely mental scrutiny" in Cottingham–Stoothoff–Murdoch but "inspection of the mind" in Haldane–Ross. Either name the edition or paraphrase |
| *Cogito, ergo sum* | The French *je pense, donc je suis* is in the *Discourse* (1637); the Latin tag is in *Principles* I.7; the *Meditations* has the first-person "I am, I exist" formulation | ✅ Safe to quote the Latin tag. ❌ Do **not** attribute "*cogito, ergo sum*" to the *Meditations* |
| Spinoza, *Ethics* (*Ethica*, posthumous 1677) | Curley, *The Collected Works of Spinoza* Vol. I (Princeton, 1985) is the current standard; Elwes (1883) and White–Stirling are the older public-domain versions widely reprinted in India | Cite by **structural reference** — I, Def. 7; I, P15; I, P16; I, P18; I, P29; II, P7; II, P40 Sch. 2; V, P32 Cor. Structural references are translation-invariant; page numbers are not |
| *Omnis determinatio est negatio* | ⚠️ **Provenance caution.** The exact Latin formula is **not** in the *Ethics*. Spinoza writes *determinatio negatio est* in **Letter 50 to Jarig Jelles, 2 June 1674**. The universalised slogan is **Hegel's** rendering | Write: "Spinoza's dictum, in Letter 50, that determination is negation — universalised by Hegel as *omnis determinatio est negatio*." This answers 2025 Q2(b) **and** earns a provenance mark |
| *Deus sive Natura* | *Ethics* IV, Preface, and IV, P4 Demonstration | ✅ Safe verbatim |
| Leibniz, *Monadology* (written 1714; printed posthumously) | Latta (1898); Ariew–Garber, *Philosophical Essays* (Hackett, 1989) | "Monads have no windows" is a standard rendering of §7 (*Les Monades n'ont point de fenêtres*), not a literal English original. ✅ Safe if you say "as it is usually rendered" |
| "The best of all possible worlds" | *Theodicy* (1710); *Monadology* §§53–55 | ✅ Safe. Note that it is Leibniz's own French, *le meilleur des mondes possibles*, and that Voltaire's *Candide* (1759) satirises it |
| "Incline without necessitating" | *Theodicy*; also the Leibniz–Clarke correspondence | ✅ Standard rendering of *incliner sans nécessiter* |

❌ **Never write:** "Descartes says the mind is a ghost in the machine." That is **Gilbert Ryle's** hostile coinage (*The Concept of Mind*, 1949). Attribute it correctly and the same sentence becomes a mark-earner.

#### 5. Answer spines

```text
  10 MARKS - D-R-A-E              about 150 to 200 words
  Define -> Reconstruct -> Apply one named text or example -> Evaluate

  15 MARKS - D-R-D-E-V            about 250 to 300 words
  Define -> Reconstruct -> Debate (objection and reply) -> Evaluate -> Verdict

  20 MARKS - C-M-C                about 340 to 400 words
  Common premise -> Mechanism of divergence -> Comparative costs and conclusion
```

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** The rationalist disagreement about mind and body is best understood as a disagreement about whether Descartes' question was well formed, and any ranking of the three answers is only as good as the criterion it declares.
> **Named evidence.** Descartes' real distinction in Meditation VI generates the problem and the correspondence with Princess Elisabeth exposes it; *Ethics* II, P7 removes it by making the two orders one; *Monadology* §7 removes it by denying influx and replacing it with pre-established harmony.
> **Analysis.** Because each successor rejects a different premise — Spinoza the plurality of substances, Leibniz the possibility of transeunt causation — the three positions are not rivals on a level field: they answer differently because they are answering differently framed questions, which is why "who is right?" must be replaced by "right by what standard?".
> **Qualification.** On explanatory economy Spinoza wins, on fidelity to lived experience Descartes wins, and on systematic consistency Leibniz wins; that no single criterion selects a unique answer is itself evidence that the original problem was generated by a metaphysical decision rather than discovered in experience.

#### Exam link

- **2024 Q3(c), 15 marks** — §1 is the whole method of the answer, and §3's mind–body row supplies the verdict.
- **2018 Q2(a), 20 marks** — the mechanism in §1 of Session 1, executed with §3's substance row.
- **2025 Q2(b), 15 marks** — §4's provenance line should be the opening sentence.
- Every question in the topic — §2's directive decoder determines the shape before a word is written.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| Comparing without a criterion | A comparison with no announced standard is a list, not an argument |
| Reading down the columns | Examiners ask you to read across the rows |
| Reporting criticism and reply without the residual | The residual column is where evaluation happens |
| Quoting the *Ethics* for "all determination is negation" | Letter 50, universalised by Hegel |
| Using Ryle's "ghost in the machine" as Descartes' own | It is a 1949 coinage by a hostile critic |
| Ignoring the directive | "Comment", "explain" and "critically discuss" demand three different shapes |

#### Revision notes

- Seven axes: substance, God, mind–body, freedom, method, the individual, the physical world.
- Criticism–reply–**residual**: always finish the third column.
- State the criterion, then rank; 2024 safe answer is Leibniz on the compatibilist criterion.
- Directives: discuss (60:40), critically discuss (evaluation dominates), comment (thesis-first), explain (derivation), substantiate (take a side), own comments (first-person quarter).
- Spines: 10 D-R-A-E; 15 D-R-D-E-V; 20 C-M-C.
- Provenance: Letter 50; Ryle 1949; *Candide* 1759; edition differences in Descartes.

#### CLOSING RECALL FLOW — COMPARISON AND ANSWER CRAFT

```text
SEVEN AXES: substance | God | mind-body | freedom | method | individual | world
        | READ ACROSS the rows, not down the columns
        v
CRITICISM -> REPLY -> RESIDUAL  (the third column is where marks are earned)
  Descartes: Circle | interaction | analogical substance
  Spinoza:   personality lost | acosmism | responsibility | negation
  Leibniz:   ad hoc harmony | "best world" | fixed complete concepts
        v
STATE THE CRITERION, THEN RANK
  alternatives possible -> DESCARTES (cost: unintelligible interaction)
  self-determination    -> SPINOZA   (cost: no free will at all)
  responsible under determination -> LEIBNIZ (cost: harmony bought in advance)
        v
DIRECTIVE DECODER: discuss | critically discuss | comment | explain |
                   substantiate | with your own comments | rank-and-defend
        v
SPINES: 10 D-R-A-E | 15 D-R-D-E-V | 20 C-M-C
        v
PROVENANCE: Letter 50 (not the Ethics) | Ryle 1949 (not Descartes) |
            Candide 1759 (satire, not doctrine) | cite Spinoza structurally
ANSWER LINE: three verdicts on whether the question was well formed - and no
             neutral criterion selects among them
```

## BASIC MCQS / REMEDIATION

### 32 original MCQs — 24 core diagnostics and 8 remedial error-targeting drills

**Answer placement:** each correct option was fixed independently, so the key sequence is unpatterned rather than rotated. Options are matched for length, specificity, grammar and visible formatting so that the answer cannot be spotted by shape alone. Every question carries four question-specific option explanations and one unique examiner trap.

**Coverage:** the twenty-four core questions run across the whole printed clause — Rationalism in Descartes, Spinoza and Leibniz; Cartesian Method and Certain Knowledge; Substance; God; Mind-Body Dualism; Determinism and Freedom — together with the three kinds of ideas, the wax argument, the Cartesian Circle, the proof of the external world, the three kinds of knowledge, striving *(conatus)*, monadic perception and hierarchy, the identity of indiscernibles, the two kinds of truth, possible worlds and pre-established harmony. The eight remedial drills attack the specific confusions that cost marks every year.

#### MCQ 1. What the rationalist thesis actually asserts

Consider the following statements about early-modern rationalism.

1. It denies that sense-experience can by itself supply the warrant for necessity and universality.
2. It holds that the structure of reality is accessible to reason, and explains that structure through substance, attribute and mode.
3. It maintains that sensory beliefs are uniformly false and that the physical world is an illusion.

Which of the statements given above are correct?

A. 1 and 3 only

B. 2 and 3 only

C. 1 and 2 only

D. 1, 2 and 3

**Answer: C.**

**Option explanations:**
- **A:** Incorrect: it couples the sound thesis about warrant with a caricature; Descartes devotes the whole of Meditation VI to *restoring* the material world rather than abolishing it.
- **B:** Incorrect: statement 2 is accurate but statement 3 attributes to the school a position Spinoza expressly rejects when he classes sense-based imagination as a genuine, if inadequate, kind of knowledge.
- **C:** Correct: the two load-bearing commitments are the claim about warrant and the claim that an intelligible reality is to be articulated through a metaphysics of substance, attribute and mode.
- **D:** Incorrect: accepting all three converts a thesis about justification into a thesis about illusion, which no member of the trio holds.

**Examiner trap 1:** Defining rationalism as hostility to the senses is the commonest opening error in this topic. Define it by the **source of warrant** for necessary truth, and the rest of the answer stays on the syllabus.

#### MCQ 2. Which rule of method does the destructive work

In the *Discourse on the Method*, Part II, Descartes states four rules. The rule that generates the wholesale demolition of Meditation I is the rule requiring him to

A. divide every difficulty under examination into as many parts as its solution requires

B. conduct his thoughts in order, beginning with the simplest and easiest objects

C. accept nothing as true which he does not clearly and evidently know to be so

D. make his enumerations so complete and his reviews so general that nothing is omitted

**Answer: C.**

**Option explanations:**
- **A:** Incorrect: analysis is a rule of problem-management inherited from mathematical practice; by itself it removes no belief whatever.
- **B:** Incorrect: proceeding from simple to complex organises an inquiry that is already under way and cannot license the suspension of whole classes of belief.
- **C:** Correct: once assent is withheld from whatever is not evident, and a single counterexample renders a whole class non-evident, the senses, the world and even mathematics can be suspended in turn.
- **D:** Incorrect: complete enumeration is a rule of review that guards against omission at the end of an inquiry, not a principle of demolition at its beginning.

**Examiner trap 2:** Listing the four rules without saying which of them does the work turns a philosophical method into a memorised quartet. Name rule one as the engine of the *Meditations*.

#### MCQ 3. The order of the three doubts

Match each stage of Cartesian doubt with what it is designed to remove.

1. Sense-deception — 2. The dream argument — 3. The evil genius *(genius malignus)*

I. Even the simple truths of arithmetic and geometry.
II. The reliability of particular perceptual judgements about how things appear.
III. The immediate perceptual situation, and with it the whole external world.

Which of the following is the correct matching?

A. 1–II, 2–III, 3–I

B. 1–III, 2–I, 3–II

C. 1–I, 2–II, 3–III

D. 1–II, 2–I, 3–III

**Answer: A.**

**Option explanations:**
- **A:** Correct: the stages are cumulative — sense-deception unsettles particular perceptual judgements, the dream argument removes the situation and the world, and only the deceiver hypothesis reaches mathematics.
- **B:** Incorrect: it makes the weakest doubt do the strongest work and leaves the deceiver hypothesis attacking what the first stage had already unsettled.
- **C:** Incorrect: it exactly inverts the sequence, so that mathematics falls first and the world falls last, destroying the argumentative escalation.
- **D:** Incorrect: it correctly places sense-deception but then reverses the two later stages, which removes the reason Descartes needs the deceiver at all.

**Examiner trap 3:** The order is itself an argument, because each stage attacks precisely what the previous stage left standing. Reporting the three doubts as an unordered list forfeits the structure the examiner is testing.

#### MCQ 4. The logical status of "I am, I exist"

Descartes' claim that he exists whenever he thinks is best characterised as

A. an inductive generalisation drawn from many observed instances of his own thinking activity

B. a deduction from the suppressed major premise that whatever thinks must exist

C. an empirical report of an inner sensation whose occurrence he happens to notice

D. an immediate intuition whose certainty is performative and confined to the present moment

**Answer: D.**

**Option explanations:**
- **A:** Incorrect: an induction would yield only a probable generalisation, whereas Descartes needs a proposition that resists a supremely powerful deceiver.
- **B:** Incorrect: Descartes explicitly denies that the *cogito* is a syllogism concealing a general premise, and says instead that it is recognised by a simple intuition of the mind.
- **C:** Incorrect: treating it as an inner observation makes it one more piece of evidence that the deceiver hypothesis could in principle contaminate.
- **D:** Correct: the certainty is generated by the act of thinking itself and holds whenever the proposition is put forward or conceived, which is exactly why deception cannot touch it.

**Examiner trap 4:** Writing "*cogito, ergo sum*" and adding "therefore it is an inference" contradicts Descartes in the same sentence. The "therefore" belongs to the Latin tag of the *Discourse* and the *Principles*, not to the logic of the *Meditations*.

#### MCQ 5. The limits of what the thinking self establishes

Consider the following statements about what the *cogito* by itself establishes.

1. That thinking is occurring, and that the thinker exists while thinking.
2. That the thinker is an enduring immaterial substance numerically identical over time.
3. That there is at least one proposition immune to the hypothesis of a powerful deceiver.

Which of the statements given above are correct?

A. 1 and 2 only

B. 1 and 3 only

C. 2 and 3 only

D. 1, 2 and 3

**Answer: B.**

**Option explanations:**
- **A:** Incorrect: statement 2 imports the substantial soul, which Descartes reaches only through the further work of Meditation II and the real distinction of Meditation VI.
- **B:** Correct: present thinking existence and immunity to the deceiver are precisely what the argument delivers, and nothing more.
- **C:** Incorrect: it pairs a genuine result with the unearned claim about diachronic identity, which is the gap Lichtenberg and Hume later exploit.
- **D:** Incorrect: accepting all three collapses the distinction between the certainty of the act and the metaphysics of its subject.

**Examiner trap 5:** Candidates routinely let the *cogito* prove the immortality of the soul in one line. Concede the narrowness of the result and then show how Descartes argues for more; the concession is worth more marks than the overreach.

#### MCQ 6. Sorting the three kinds of ideas

Match each class of idea with its correct description.

1. Adventitious *(adventitiae)* — 2. Factitious *(factitiae)* — 3. Innate *(innatae)*

I. Native to the mind's own rational nature and elicited on an appropriate occasion.
II. Received as though from outside, arriving involuntarily through a passive faculty.
III. Constructed voluntarily by the mind out of ideas it already possesses.

Which of the following is the correct matching?

A. 1–II, 2–III, 3–I

B. 1–I, 2–II, 3–III

C. 1–III, 2–I, 3–II

D. 1–II, 2–I, 3–III

**Answer: A.**

**Option explanations:**
- **A:** Correct: adventitious ideas arrive involuntarily, factitious ideas are built by the mind at will, and innate ideas belong to the intellect's constitution.
- **B:** Incorrect: it makes the involuntary class native and the native class constructed, which destroys the causal argument of Meditation III.
- **C:** Incorrect: it treats made-up ideas as involuntary arrivals, so the golden mountain would need an external cause of its own.
- **D:** Incorrect: it correctly identifies the first class but exchanges the other two, so voluntary construction becomes native endowment.

**Examiner trap 6:** The classification is not decorative. It exists so that the idea of God can be placed in the third class and its content argued to exceed what a finite mind could manufacture.

#### MCQ 7. What Descartes means by an innate idea

The strongest formulation of Cartesian innateness — the one that Locke's appeal to the absence of universal assent does not reach — is that an innate idea is

A. a proposition consciously affirmed by every human being from the earliest moment of life

B. a sensory impression so frequently repeated that it has become impossible to doubt

C. an image stored in the memory before birth and later recovered by a distinct act of recollection

D. a native disposition of the intellect which produces the idea on an appropriate occasion

**Answer: D.**

**Option explanations:**
- **A:** Incorrect: this is the occurrent version of the doctrine, and it is exactly the version Locke destroys by pointing to children who affirm no such principles.
- **B:** Incorrect: grounding the idea in repeated sensation concedes the empiricist's central claim and abandons the rationalist thesis about warrant.
- **C:** Incorrect: pre-natal storage and recollection belong to the Platonic tradition; Descartes speaks of seeds of knowledge in the mind's constitution, not of retrieved images.
- **D:** Correct: innateness is dispositional — the mind is so constituted that, given an occasion, it produces the idea from its own resources.

**Examiner trap 7:** A 2023-style answer that presents the occurrent version and then reports Locke's refutation has staged a contest in which one side never got up. Always state the dispositional version first.

#### MCQ 8. Locke's grounds of refutation

Consider the following as grounds on which Locke rejects innate principles in the *Essay concerning Human Understanding*, Book I.

1. Alleged innate principles command no universal assent, since children and others do not affirm them.
2. Nothing can be said to be in the mind and yet unknown to it, on pain of making every knowable truth innate.
3. The idea of infinity is demonstrably richer than anything the mind could ever construct from finite materials.

Which of the statements given above are correct?

A. 1 and 3 only

B. 1 and 2 only

C. 2 and 3 only

D. 1, 2 and 3

**Answer: B.**

**Option explanations:**
- **A:** Incorrect: statement 3 is a Cartesian premise for innateness, not a Lockean objection to it; Locke argues the opposite, that infinity is reached by repetition and negation.
- **B:** Correct: the absence of universal assent and the transparency of mental content are Locke's two principal tests, supported by his positive derivation of ideas from sensation and reflection.
- **C:** Incorrect: it pairs a genuine Lockean test with a claim that belongs to the side Locke is attacking.
- **D:** Incorrect: including statement 3 makes Locke argue for the doctrine he devotes Book I to dismantling.

**Examiner trap 8:** Omitting **reflection** misstates Locke. His two sources of ideas are sensation *and* the mind's observation of its own operations, and a script that mentions only sensation invites an easy correction.

#### MCQ 9. What the wax argument establishes

The conclusion the wax argument of Meditation II is designed to secure is that

A. the wax has no real properties at all once its sensible qualities have been destroyed by heat

B. bodily identity through qualitative change is grasped by the intellect rather than by sense

C. the senses are wholly inoperative and contribute nothing whatever to any human judgement

D. the existence of external material bodies follows directly from the persistence of the wax

**Answer: B.**

**Option explanations:**
- **A:** Incorrect: the wax retains extension, flexibility and changeability; the argument removes the sensible catalogue, not the object.
- **B:** Correct: every item in the sensible catalogue is replaced while the same wax is still judged to remain, so the vehicle of that judgement cannot be sense.
- **C:** Incorrect: Descartes' point is that what passes for seeing is judging, not that seeing has ceased; the "hats and coats" case makes exactly this distinction.
- **D:** Incorrect: existence is restored only in Meditation VI through divine veracity, and confusing the two is among the most heavily penalised errors in this topic.

**Examiner trap 9:** The wax proves *how* body would be known, never *that* body exists. Saying so explicitly, and in one sentence, is itself worth a mark in the 2019-style question.

#### MCQ 10. Why imagination cannot do the work

In the wax argument, the reason Descartes rejects imagination as the faculty by which the wax is grasped is that

A. imagination is always accompanied by a sensory impression and so inherits every sensory defect

B. imagination belongs to the body and is therefore extinguished whenever the body is altered

C. heat can give the wax indefinitely many shapes, which no finite run of images could ever exhaust

D. imagination operates only on objects that are absent, whereas the wax is present to him

**Answer: C.**

**Option explanations:**
- **A:** Incorrect: the argument does not turn on imagination inheriting sensory defects; it turns on a mismatch between an indefinite range and a finite capacity.
- **B:** Incorrect: nothing in the passage makes imagination cease when the wax is heated, and the claim would prove far more than Descartes needs.
- **C:** Correct: this is the force of "flexible and changeable" *(flexibile et mutabile)* — the possible shapes are indefinite in number and cannot be run through in images.
- **D:** Incorrect: the presence or absence of the object is irrelevant, since the argument concerns the range of possible forms rather than their availability to sense.

**Examiner trap 10:** Answers that jump from "not sense" straight to "therefore intellect" skip the step that does the real work. Imagination must be eliminated separately, and by the indefiniteness of the possible shapes.

#### MCQ 11. Matching the proofs of God to their strategy

Match each Cartesian proof of God with its distinctive argumentative move.

1. Trademark or causal — 2. Conservation — 3. Ontological

I. Existence is contained in the concept of a supremely perfect being, so its denial is a contradiction.
II. I cannot sustain my own existence from one moment to the next, so a continuing cause is required.
III. The content of my idea of an infinite being exceeds anything a finite mind could have produced.

Which of the following is the correct matching?

A. 1–III, 2–II, 3–I

B. 1–I, 2–III, 3–II

C. 1–II, 2–I, 3–III

D. 1–III, 2–I, 3–II

**Answer: A.**

**Option explanations:**
- **A:** Correct: the causal proof works from the objective reality of the idea, the conservation proof from my momentary dependence, and the ontological proof from the concept alone.
- **B:** Incorrect: it makes the trademark proof conceptual and the ontological proof causal, reversing the very feature that distinguishes them.
- **C:** Incorrect: it assigns momentary dependence to the trademark proof and conceptual necessity to conservation, so no proof retains its own premise.
- **D:** Incorrect: it opens correctly but then swaps conservation with the ontological route, losing the division of labour among the three arguments.

**Examiner trap 11:** Naming the division of labour among the three proofs earns more than reciting all three at length. Each answers a different question — where the idea came from, what sustains me, and what the concept contains.

#### MCQ 12. The Cartesian Circle and the reply

Arnauld's objection in the Fourth Objections, and Descartes' reply to it, are best stated as follows:

A. the objection is that God cannot be proved at all, and the reply is that the idea of God is innate rather than voluntarily made

B. the objection is that the *cogito* is really a syllogism, and the reply is that it is an immediate intuition

C. the objection is that the senses cannot ever be trusted, and the reply is that a perfect God would never deceive

D. the objection is that the criterion proves God who certifies it, and the reply separates attended from remembered perception

**Answer: D.**

**Option explanations:**
- **A:** Incorrect: innateness answers a question about the origin of the idea, not the charge that the proof presupposes what it establishes.
- **B:** Incorrect: this correctly describes a different exchange about the logical form of the *cogito*, which Arnauld's circularity charge does not concern.
- **C:** Incorrect: divine veracity is the conclusion under attack, so citing it as the reply simply restates the position Arnauld is challenging.
- **D:** Correct: the charge is circularity between criterion and guarantee, and Descartes replies that a perception actually before the mind compels assent while God secures memory and extended reasoning.
 
**Examiner trap 12:** The reply must be followed by its residual weakness: the proofs of God are themselves extended arguments whose earlier premises are remembered rather than attended, so the circle is softened rather than removed.

#### MCQ 13. The proof of the external world

Consider the following steps in Descartes' Meditation VI argument for material things.

1. Sensory ideas arrive involuntarily, so I possess a passive faculty of receiving them.
2. I have a great natural inclination to refer these ideas to bodies, and no faculty by which the inclination could be corrected.
3. The wax argument has already demonstrated that at least one extended body exists outside me.

Which of the statements given above are correct?

A. 1 and 3 only

B. 2 and 3 only

C. 1 and 2 only

D. 1, 2 and 3

**Answer: C.**

**Option explanations:**
- **A:** Incorrect: statement 3 misassigns to the wax argument an existential conclusion it expressly does not reach.
- **B:** Incorrect: it retains the correct claim about natural inclination but again treats the wax as an existence proof.
- **C:** Correct: involuntariness establishes a passive faculty needing an active cause, and the uncorrectable inclination makes any alternative attribution a divine deception.
- **D:** Incorrect: including statement 3 imports a conclusion from Meditation II that Meditation VI is written precisely to supply.

**Examiner trap 13:** Compressing this into "God guarantees the world" throws away the whole question. The 2019 examiner is marking a five-step causal argument, not a slogan.

#### MCQ 14. The argument for the real distinction

The reasoning by which Descartes concludes that mind and body are really distinct proceeds by holding that

A. whatever can be clearly and distinctly conceived apart can by God's power exist apart, and so is distinct

B. whatever is observed to behave independently in the course of ordinary experience must be a separate substance

C. whatever has a different location in the body must belong to a different kind of created thing

D. whatever survives the hypothesis of the evil genius is thereby shown to be immaterial in nature

**Answer: A.**

**Option explanations:**
- **A:** Correct: the inference runs from clear and distinct separate conception, through divine omnipotence, to real separability and hence real distinction.
- **B:** Incorrect: observed independence is precisely the sort of empirical premise the *Meditations* has already suspended, and it could not yield a metaphysical conclusion.
- **C:** Incorrect: location is a property of extended things alone, so an argument from location cannot establish that anything unextended exists.
- **D:** Incorrect: surviving the deceiver shows that the *cogito* is indubitable, not that its subject is immaterial; that further claim needs the separability argument.

**Examiner trap 14:** Dualism is a **conclusion** of the Cartesian system, drawn from the criterion of truth and divine omnipotence. Presenting it as an unargued starting assumption misrepresents the architecture and loses the link to Session 5.

#### MCQ 15. The interaction problem and the pineal gland

Descartes' appeal to the pineal gland, and Princess Elisabeth's objection to his position, are best understood as follows:

A. the gland is the seat of the soul, a claim Elisabeth accepts while denying that any such soul can be immortal

B. the gland gives interaction a location, while Elisabeth asks how the unextended could move the extended

C. the gland is the organ of imagination, which Elisabeth argues should instead be assigned to the intellect

D. the gland dissolves the problem entirely, and Elisabeth's objection concerns only the physiology of the nerves

**Answer: B.**

**Option explanations:**
- **A:** Incorrect: immortality is not the issue in the 1643 correspondence, which turns on the intelligibility of causal commerce between the two substances.
- **B:** Correct: Descartes offers a site, chosen because the gland is unpaired and central, whereas Elisabeth's question is about a mechanism, which no site can supply.
- **C:** Incorrect: the dispute is not about which faculty is housed where, but about how any causal influence across the real distinction is possible.
- **D:** Incorrect: Descartes himself does not claim that the gland dissolves the difficulty; his considered reply is that the union is a primitive notion.
 
**Examiner trap 15:** Treating the pineal gland as Descartes' answer misses his real answer. The philosophical reply is that the mind–body union is a third **primitive notion**, known through ordinary living rather than through pure intellect.

#### MCQ 16. Error and the grades of freedom in Meditation IV

According to Descartes' account of error and freedom, error arises because the will is

A. finite while the intellect is infinite, so that understanding constantly outruns what may be affirmed

B. determined by bodily motions, so that assent is compelled by mechanical causes outside the mind

C. most free when it is wholly indifferent, since indifference alone leaves alternatives genuinely open

D. infinite in scope while the intellect is finite, so that assent may be given beyond clear perception

**Answer: D.**

**Option explanations:**
- **A:** Incorrect: it exactly reverses the two faculties, and on that reversal error could never occur, since nothing would be affirmed beyond what is understood.
- **B:** Incorrect: making assent mechanically compelled would remove the responsibility for error that Meditation IV is written to establish.
- **C:** Incorrect: Descartes ranks indifference as the *lowest* grade of freedom, since the will is most free when moved by clearly perceived truth.
- **D:** Correct: the infinite will can affirm or deny anything, the finite intellect perceives clearly only within limits, and error is assent given outside those limits.

**Examiner trap 16:** The claim that indifference is the highest freedom is the reverse of Descartes' text. Getting the ranking right also strengthens the later contrast with Spinoza, for whom freedom likewise increases with adequate understanding.

#### MCQ 17. Spinoza's argument for one substance

The demonstration by which Spinoza reaches a single substance turns essentially on the premise that

A. two substances cannot share an attribute, so nothing whatever could distinguish them as substances

B. God is defined at the outset as the only being whose essence involves its own existence

C. extension is divisible and therefore cannot belong to what is ultimately real in nature

D. thought and extension are known to us, while infinitely many further attributes remain unknown

**Answer: A.**

**Option explanations:**
- **A:** Correct: from the impossibility of shared attributes it follows that a finite substance would need limiting by one of the same nature, so substance must be infinite and unique.
- **B:** Incorrect: self-causation is established in the course of the argument rather than stipulated, and stipulating it would make the demonstration circular.
- **C:** Incorrect: this is Leibniz's objection to extended substance, and Spinoza retains Extension as an attribute expressing the whole divine essence.
- **D:** Incorrect: the limitation of human knowledge to two attributes is a consequence of the system, not a premise from which uniqueness could be derived.

**Examiner trap 17:** Asserting monism without the no-shared-attribute step reduces a demonstration to a slogan. The 2022 and 2024 questions both reward the derivation rather than the conclusion.

#### MCQ 18. Attributes, modes and the contrast with Descartes

For Spinoza, thought and extension are best described as

A. two finite modes which together exhaust the content of the one infinite substance

B. two created substances which depend for their existence on the infinite substance

C. two attributes, each expressing the whole eternal essence of the one substance

D. two aspects of human perception which do not correspond to anything in substance

**Answer: C.**

**Option explanations:**
- **A:** Incorrect: modes are affections existing in another and conceived through another, whereas an attribute is what the intellect perceives as constituting substance's essence.
- **B:** Incorrect: this is precisely the Cartesian position Spinoza rejects, and importing it collapses the whole point of difference between the two systems.
- **C:** Correct: each attribute expresses the whole essence, not a portion of it, and substance possesses infinitely many of which we know these two.
- **D:** Incorrect: reducing attributes to features of human perception makes them subjective, whereas Spinoza needs them to be real expressions of substance.

**Examiner trap 18:** Confusing Spinoza's **attributes** with Descartes' **substances** is the single most damaging error in comparative questions, because it destroys the explanation of why parallelism replaces interaction.

#### MCQ 19. "Whatever is, is in God"

In *Ethics* I, P15 and P18 together, the claim that whatever is, is in God, is best understood as asserting that

A. finite things are spatially contained within a divine body that extends throughout the whole universe

B. finite things exist as modes of the one substance, whose immanent and not transitive cause is God

C. finite things were produced by an act of will at a moment in time and are conserved by divine choice

D. finite things are appearances with no reality of their own, since only the infinite is genuinely real

**Answer: B.**

**Option explanations:**
- **A:** Incorrect: the containment is ontological rather than spatial, and a spatial reading would make God a very large extended object among others.
- **B:** Correct: modes exist in and are conceived through substance, and P18's immanent causation means the cause remains in the effect rather than producing something outside itself.
- **C:** Incorrect: Spinoza denies deliberation and temporal creation; modes follow from what substance is, not from a discretionary decree.
